new KL Before and After Mixing: A Round-Complexity Separation in Parallel Sampling

Authors: Tianyi Wu

Abstract: A parallel sampler can choose at random which coordinates to generate together. For a target $p$ and the output law $q_U$ of a schedule $U$ drawn afresh for each output, we compare the schedule-averaged divergence $\mathbb{E}_U D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p\|q_U)$ with the output-law divergence $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p\|\mathbb{E}_U q_U)$, in nats. Rounds commit independent draws from exact conditional marginals frozen at the round's start, each output's schedule is drawn before sampling from a finite mixture of schedules, and every scheduled round is charged. For the uniform distribution on binary strings of even length $n$ with $n/2$ ones, the least expected number of rounds at a fixed tolerance $\varepsilon>0$ is $\Theta_\varepsilon((\log n)^2)$ under the first criterion but $(e^{-\varepsilon}+o(1))\log_2\ln n$ under the second, and the double-logarithmic order is attainable with the same round count on every run. For this target, a count-state recursion gives the exact least output divergence at every round cap over all causal randomized policies. The separation extends to weighted fixed-count targets with bounded weight ratios and quota fractions bounded away from zero and one, without permutation symmetry, and to the full-support Beta$(1,1)$--Bernoulli target, where the sharp expected cost under the output-law criterion is $(e^{-\varepsilon}/2+o(1))\log_2\log_2n$, attained with rare $n$-round runs. Prior work implies the order of the schedule-averaged cost on the balanced target and its $O_\varepsilon((\log n)^2)$ upper bounds on both extensions.

new AI Models Can Provably Hide Arbitrary Capabilities

Authors: Andis Draguns, Sumeet Ramesh Motwani, Raymond Douglas, Christian Schroeder de Witt

Abstract: Capability evaluations aim to surface dangerous model behaviors before deployment, but their reliability depends on the assumption that hidden capabilities can be elicited. We show this assumption does not hold under adversarial conditions by constructing backdoor attacks that embed encrypted neural networks within host model weights, decrypting and executing them only upon receiving a secret trigger. Using digital lockers, these hidden circuits remain provably hard to elicit and interpret under cryptographic assumptions, even given full access to model weights, extending prior work from hiding fixed strings to arbitrary computations. We implement our proof of concept constructions in PyTorch and evaluate elicitation through fine-tuning, weight noising, gradient-based trigger search, and latent adversarial training on a small chemical reaction prediction task - a proxy for CBRN-relevant capabilities. Our results demonstrate resistance to these methods and, together with our cryptographic guarantees, reveal a limit of elicitation-based capability audits. We release our models to support the improvement of defenses.

cross A Sampling Complexity-aware Framework for Discrete-time Fractional-Order Dynamical System Identification

Authors: Xiaole Zhang, Vijay Gupta, Paul Bogdan

Abstract: A variety of complex biological, natural and man-made systems exhibit non-Markovian dynamics that can be modeled through fractional order differential equations, yet, we lack sample comlexity aware system identification strategies. Towards this end, we propose an affine discrete-time fractional order dynamical system (FoDS) identification algorithm and provide a detailed sample complexity analysis. The algorithm effectively addresses the challenges of FoDS identification in the presence of noisy data. The proposed algorithm consists of two key steps. Firstly, it avoids solving higher-order polynomial equations, which would otherwise result in multiple potential solutions for the fractional orders. Secondly, the identification problem is reformulated as a least squares estimation, allowing us to infer the system parameters. We derive the expectation and probabilistic bounds for the FoDS parameter estimation error, assuming prior knowledge of the functions \( f \) and \( g \) in the FoDS model. The error decays at a rate of \( N = O\left( \frac{d}{\epsilon} \right) \), where \( N \) is the number of samples, \( d \) is the dimension of the state variable, and \( \epsilon \) represents the desired estimation accuracy. Simulation results demonstrate that our theoretical bounds are tight, validating the accuracy and robustness of this algorithm.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models increasingly incorporate intermediate reasoning to improve robotic manipulation, yet existing approaches primarily reason about observed states without explicitly anticipating future scene evolution. cross On the estimation and validity of AI time horizons---a statistical look at the METR plot

Authors: Drew T. Nguyen, William Fithian

Abstract: METR's 50\% time horizon measures the human completion time of software tasks that an AI solves with 50\% probability, allowing AI capabilities to be expressed in interpretable units. On 228 tasks and 26 AIs, we recompute the time horizons using splines and item-response theory to relax the assumption that the AI difficulty of a task depends linearly on the log of human time. Our fitted spline can be interpreted as a function that \emph{converts} human time to AI difficulty; it is nearly flat in a region from 2--30 min but close to linear elsewhere. Hence, a time-horizon jump from 3 min to 30 min is much easier than one from 30 min to 5 hours despite the same multiplier of $10 \times$. Overall, we contribute time-horizon point estimates that perform better under a cross-validated suite of proper scoring rules, as well as diagnostic plots for assessing time horizons' construct validity. We suggest that time horizons be interpreted together with the diagnostic plots, especially as new time-horizon-based benchmarks are proposed or existing ones grow to include longer tasks.

Authors: Suresh Kumar TP, Vikas Agnihotri

Abstract: Satellite onboard computing faces an irreducible tension: mission intelligence requirements grow monotonically while power, mass, and radiation budgets remain fixed. cross TTS-Q: Perception-Guided Post-Training Quantization of Text-to-Speech Diffusion Transformers

Authors: Xiaoqin Feng, Yuqi Li, Weilun Feng, Yi-Cheng Lin, Kai Li, Xianglong Wang, Kuo Yang, Chuanguang Yang, Yingli Tian, Wei Li, Zhongwei Cheng, Lin Chen

Abstract: Zero-shot text-to-speech (TTS) can clone a voice from seconds of audio. Yet the quality comes from billion-parameter diffusion transformers whose size and multi-step sampling put them out of reach of the on-device, assistive, and real-time applications that need them most. Post-training quantization (PTQ) shrinks such models without retraining, but it has been built for language and vision, not speech. At 4 bits, a strong quantizer leaves intelligibility close to full precision but consistently erodes speaker timbre, so error rates stay low while the voice drifts from the target speaker. We ask why quantization hurts speech, where the damage lives, and whether it can be repaired without retraining. To find out, we run the first 4-bit weight-and-activation (W4A4) PTQ study of a TTS diffusion transformer at 1B and 3.5B. The timbre cost comes from both the weight and the activation quantization, but only the activation part has the timestep structure a precision schedule can exploit. We therefore introduce TTS-Q, which selects hard-to-quantize items from task-matched calibration candidates and applies a budget-balanced mixed-precision schedule that assigns higher precision to late-step feed-forward activations. TTS-Q recovers timbre best at both scales, at no cost to intelligibility. Separately, the deployed calibrated-W4A4 reference reduces peak memory on GPU, providing a practical reference for resource-constrained TTS deployment.

cross Faithfulness Without Anchors: A Position Paper on the Structural Misalignments of Explainable AI Faithfulness Metrics for Feature Importance

Authors: Marco Zullich, Emily Schiller, Ivan Gentile, David Dembinsky, Adriano Lucieri, Kilian G\"oller, Steffen Seitz

Abstract: Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) tools have often been presented as methods to increase trust in machine learning models by making aspects of their algorithms more human-understandable. However, evidence accumulated over the last decade points to an overall failure of this objective, instead promoting overreliance on incorrect predictions, which can subsequently lead to algorithmic aversion. Rather, XAI appears to function primarily as a debugging tool, useful for obtaining anecdotal insights into, for example, features learned by the model. One of the main issues underlying this limitation is that XAI methods often oversimplify the inner workings of the model, resulting in misalignment between the explanation and the model itself - a behavior referred to as unfaithfulness or infidelity. Faithfulness can be interpreted as the degree of approximation between an explanation and the model. Computing faithfulness in a rigorous manner would thus make it possible to quantify one of the various elements of trustworthiness of an explanation. However, existing metrics present significant limitations, making this quantification either infeasible or computationally expensive. In this position paper, we outline five major shortcomings of current faithfulness metrics and propose four possible avenues for research to bolster their reliability.

cross EarLog: Opportunistic Ear Physiology Sensing toward Personalized Hearing Risk Awareness

Authors: Yongzhi Huang, Jiayi Zhao, Kaishun Wu

Abstract: Many headphone users risk irreversible hearing damage during high-load listening, yet current protection still relies on sound-pressure limits. Clinical tests detect damage after it becomes measurable, leaving the risk-accumulating process unobserved. The acoustic reflex (AR), an involuntary response to high acoustic load, marks when the ear enters a protective state. AR traces are weak, and existing measurements require dedicated probes and controlled conditions that are difficult to maintain during everyday audio. We present EarLog, which turns playback, the dominant interference, into an opportunistic probe. EarLog achieves 99.0% sensitivity and 98.7% specificity on a clinically referenced benchmark. cross CORAL-KV: Compact Cross-Layer Prediction and Residual Coding for Autoregressive Video KV Caches

Authors: Albert Ge, Akash Haridas, Utkarsh Saxena, Ruijing Yang, Ian Colbert, Frederic Sala, Vikram Appia, Emad Barsoum

Abstract: Autoregressive video diffusion models maintain a growing attention key-value (KV) cache, making memory a key bottleneck for long video generation. Recently, clustering-based quantization has emerged as an effective method to compress video KV caches by grouping similar key and value vectors then quantizing residual errors. However, when later frames attend to the compressed history, small reconstruction errors can compound as the video grows. Beyond the within-layer similarity exploited by clustering, we find strong dependencies across cache layers in video models. This motivates Coral-KV - Calibrated Offline, Residual-coded, and Adapted Locally - a KV-cache compression framework that uses cache states in each layer to predict keys and values in subsequent layers, then quantizes the remaining prediction residuals. We construct compact block-diagonal-plus-low-rank (block-DPLR) predictors: block-diagonal components model channel relationships within each attention head, while a global low-rank term models dependencies between heads. Coral-KV exposes a flexible fidelity-memory trade-off: it can reuse its predictors across videos or refit lightweight block-diagonal predictors to the current video for higher fidelity. Across extensive evaluations on both HY-WorldPlay and MAGI-1, our reusable configuration improves PSNR over QVG-4 by 0.32 dB and 4.95 dB, respectively, while using 48% and 37% less cache memory. Against QVG-Pro, our video-adapted configuration improves MAGI-1 PSNR by 3.77 dB while using 33.7% less cache memory. Coral-KV also maintains higher reconstruction fidelity over extended generation and reduces temporal discontinuities while retaining comparable generation latency.

cross Looking Under the Streetlight: Evaluation in Generative Molecular Dynamics

Authors: Simon Olsson, Frank No\'e, Grant M. Rotskoff, Kresten Lindorff-Larsen

Abstract: This position paper argues that the generative molecular dynamics (GenMD) community is not properly evaluating its progress: current evaluations let a model match what is reported while being arbitrarily wrong on what was claimed, which risks over-claiming advantage over classical baselines. This reflects a structural misalignment between common evaluation practices and the scientific tasks the field sets for itself. The reason is that the ground truth---exhaustive molecular dynamics on the systems of interest---is computationally costly, and this cost is what motivates the field. With ground truth inaccessible, evaluation drifts toward convenient proxies, and the resulting norms entrench easily. We identify misaligned evaluation patterns that persist as a community-level equilibrium, and propose concrete observables, reporting standards, and benchmark designs, both computational and experimental, that better reflect what GenMD aspires to deliver. Our aim is to give the field shared language and tools for evaluation that match its scientific ambitions before today's conventions calcify into norms.

cross MolP2SBench: Do LLMs Really Reason from Molecular Properties to Structure?

Authors: Yuhang Xie, Zhiyi Shi, Zeyi Zhang, Zhisheng Qi, Yushun Dong, Yu Wang, Xiongye Xiao

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in molecular discovery and structure elucidation, where decisions depend on physical observations. Existing evaluations primarily focus on end-to-end molecular reconstruction, where correct predictions do not necessarily indicate reliance on the supplied properties. We introduce MolP2SBench, a benchmark for molecular property-to-structure (P2S) reasoning in LLMs. Each task presents a masked molecular scaffold with given properties and asks the model to select the missing structure from plausible candidates. LLM performance also need not reflect the relative difficulty of these properties for human chemists. Although UV-Vis spectra provide less direct structural information than NMR spectra, Astra achieves higher accuracy with UV-Vis data than with NMR peaks. MolP2SBench supports studying how LLMs extract and use physical-property information, revealing patterns that may differ from conventional chemical intuition and providing a foundation for more reliably property-grounded models for molecular and quantum-chemical inference. cross Structural and Representational Agency in LLM-Driven Timbre Generation and Interpolation

Authors: Joseph Cameron, Alan Blackwell

Abstract: Generative creative interfaces routinely report the user's position within a space of possibilities. Such displays imply that the quantities shown were measured from the artefact; in a growing class of systems they are instead predicted by a language model describing its own output, and the two are indistinguishable on screen. Most creative domains lack an agreed measurement against which such a self-report can be checked; audio admits one. In a measurement study of a working LLM-driven timbre interface, what a user can cause proves separable from what the interface reports. The first is exactly characterisable: the reachable set is a zonotope whose dimension increases by one per sound placed, so the space the model may act within is demonstrably the user's own. The second is inaccurate. The displayed position differs from measurement by approximately three times the width of that region, and the error has two independent sources: model-predicted coordinates, whose accuracy varies twofold between the two models evaluated, and the interpolation step, which arises from combining synthesiser parameters and is unaffected by model quality. End to end, 26 of 36 refinement instructions produced no perceptible change, and under the weaker model the facility performed no action while reporting convergence. Effective agency (what a user can cause) and epistemic agency (whether they can know it) are therefore separable and independently engineered: structurally enforced constraints survive model failure, whereas guarantees resting on the model's own report do not. The failure mode that remains is silence presented as success. We close with a short protocol for building and auditing such displays.

cross Equal-Budget Monte Carlo Tree Search versus Randomized-Restart List Scheduling for DAG Workflows: A Controlled Equal-Budget Computational Study

Authors: Yuyu Zhang

Abstract: Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) is often proposed for scheduling directed-acyclic-graph (DAG) workflows, yet most studies never run the comparison that could refute it - giving a much simpler method the same search effort. We run that comparison under a controlled protocol. All arms receive an identical budget - the same number of candidate schedules built and evaluated: an MCTS scheduling method, preregistered as the main arm; a randomized-restart list scheduler with no search tree as the control; and a textbook-UCT MCTS arm as a registered follow-up on the same domain. At the main budget of 2048 evaluated schedules, MCTS is 2.88% worse than the restarts (95% CI [-3.62%,-2.15%]; the 72 of 96 held-out instances with positive baseline-relative headroom). The deficit is not budget starvation: on the separate exploration pool restarts self-improve by 1.85% where MCTS gains 0.24%, and 700 of 720 of MCTS's first results are merely the warm start it was handed. The studied selection rule locks deterministically onto the first-expanded child, keeping its trials correlated while restarts draw gains from the tails; the causal link to makespan remains an inference. A structural map locates where the tree stops paying: restarts win on sparse irregular workflows, no difference is detectable on regular layered ones, and dense ones leave no room to improve. Swapping the rule for textbook UCT removes most of the deficit (-0.26%, 95% CI [-0.71%,+0.23%]) without reversing its sign. cross AutoIndexer: Flexible-Order Decoding by Training Causal Models on Chains of Edits

Authors: Shu Ishida, Aliasghar Khani, Tianyu Zhang, The Cong Luong, James Seale Smith, Adam Gaier

Abstract: Autoregressive decoding is less suited to non-causally ordered data, e.g., cross-references or future dependencies, since their outputs cannot be revised post hoc, unlike iterative human reasoning. Diffusion LLMs partially mitigate this limitation but still cannot flexibly insert or remove arbitrary numbers of tokens from a pre-generated context. We demonstrate that our method exhibits post-generation context-editing capabilities that most autoregressive and diffusion models lack without compromising performance on standard LLM benchmarks. The code is available at https://github.com/AutodeskAILab/autoindexer.

cross Fly-by-Code: Embodied Coding Agents for Aerial Manipulation with Active Visual and Physical Feedback

Authors: Jaewoo Lee, Jeongyeon Seo, Sihyun Cho, Gyeongrak Choe, Yutong Wang, Bavin Saravanan, Jia-Bin Huang, Furong Huang, Sebastian Scherer, Guanya Shi, H. Jin Kim, Seungjae Lee, Dongjae Lee

cross SpireBench: Evaluating Long-Horizon Strategic Play of Agentic Models with Slay the Spire

Authors: Scott C. Lowe, Sander W. Keemink

Abstract: Agentic large language models now tackle complex tasks, yet strategic reasoning over long horizons is under-studied: most agentic benchmarks are short and episodic, and performance on long tasks is hard to dissect into stored knowledge, reasoning and luck. cross MagicBeanStalk: Escaping the RLVR Zero Advantage Trap with Recursive Data Synthesis

Authors: Ximing Lu, Aviral Kumar, Jan Kautz, Yejin Choi, David Acuna

Abstract: Data-centric scaling of RLVR has focused on collecting or generating verifiable training problems. However, corpus size alone does not determine whether the dataset provides a useful learning signal for a target policy. To guide the synthesis of effective RLVR data, we experimentally characterize dataset effectiveness as a function of its problems, the target policy, and the training rollout budget. Under this characterization, we show that a large open-source math corpus pooled from widely used RLVR datasets falls almost entirely into a zero-advantage trap: for a fixed target policy and rollout budget, roughly 88% of its problems are solved in every rollout or failed in every rollout. Filtering concentrates what remains, but it can only select, discarding the hard problems. Effective synthetic problems should therefore require the reasoning operations and compositions required to solve those hard problems. Yet their difficulty must be calibrated, so that some rollouts succeed, some fail, and at least one correct solution fits within the training budget. Prompting a teacher to vary hard problems directly does not achieve this. Guided by these empirical principles, we introduce MagicBeanStalk, a top-down decomposition and bottom-up recursive data-synthesis algorithm that grows seed problems into families of substantively new, policy- and budget-calibrated training problems while preserving the seeds' reasoning blueprints. Across 8 math benchmarks, continual RLVR on MagicBeanStalk problems improves gpt-oss-120b from 61.2 to 69.8 (+8.6) and DeepSeek v4Flash from 72.4 to 77.9 (+5.4) on a 16K-token budget. Training on the full 78K source corpus improves by only +1.1 and its best filtered real data subset by +5.8. In finance, we synthesize over 14K verifiable problems from only 340 solutions and improve over base by 6.9 and 2.4 points on SecQue and FinanceBench, respectively.

Abstract: This paper studies an open network design problem of constructing graph topologies with maximum algebraic connectivity under fixed numbers of vertices and edges. cross Product Monte Carlo: Expectation Under a Product of Densities

Authors: Pietro Carlotti, Sifan Liu, Yuling Yao

Abstract: Aggregating independently computed sub-sample inferences is a well-known challenge in large-scale Bayesian computation, federated learning, and opinion pooling. The core problem is to approximate a distribution whose density is proportional to a product of individual densities. We propose Product Monte Carlo (PMC), an algorithm for computing arbitrary expectations under a product of densities using only Monte Carlo draws from individual densities. We turn the intractable product expectation into a regression problem by expressing it through a family of computable conditional expectations. Besides the point estimate, our fully Bayesian regression formulation provides coherent uncertainty quantification and principled diagnostics. We establish theoretical guarantees on consistency and characterize convergence rates for the PMC estimator. Across simulated and real-data experiments varying the number, dimensionality, and heterogeneity of the component distributions, PMC achieves higher estimation accuracy, better-calibrated uncertainty coverage, and improved scalability at a lower computational cost than existing methods. In addition, PMC requires no density evaluation, making it applicable to simulation-based inference, as we demonstrate in an application with protein images.

cross Bridging Human and Model Attention: Explainability Analysis of CNN, Mamba, and ViT Architectures with Gaze-Based Validation

Authors: Marco Stefani, Edoardo Parpaiola, Matteo Bino, Gianluca Caregnato, Francesco Roncolato, Daniel Fusaro, Loris Nanni, Alberto Pretto

Abstract: High-risk applications of deep learning require precise characterization of model decision-making. We conduct a systematic explainability evaluation across Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), Vision Transformers (ViTs), and Vision Mamba (ViM). Unlike predominantly qualitative studies, we quantify the alignment between model attention and human visual perception using eye-tracking ground truth. We formalize this comparison via a standardized normalization pipeline mapping post-hoc attribution maps and gaze heatmaps onto spatial probability distributions. The results highlight how architectural and training choices influence the alignment between model saliency and human visual attention.

cross PVBRF-ID: Private, Verifiable and Byzantine-Robust Federated Intrusion Detection for Oilfield Enterprise Networks

Authors: Mustafa S. Aljumaily, Ali Ataeemh Allami, Mustafa N. Mnati, Nawar S. Alseelawi, Shahab Abdulla

Abstract: Federated learning allows dispersed oilfield sites to train a shared intrusion detector without pooling network traffic, yet its aggregation step forces a choice between privacy and robustness. Byzantine-robust rules must read each update in the clear, whereas secure aggregation hides updates from the checks that detect poisoning. We present PVBRF-ID, a two-server protocol in which sites secret-share their updates and the servers jointly test each update's norm and its cosine similarity to a reference computed on a small clean root set, revealing one accept bit per site. Backdoors passed the filter, and noise-based mitigation gave only heuristic protection. The implementation uses Python 3.13, NumPy 2.3, 30 rounds and 10 seeds.

Abstract: Large language models are stochastic by nature. Repeated executions under the same experimental conditions may produce different responses. Abstract: Stereo vision is increasingly used for robotic manipulation, with a growing body of work incorporating it into Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. cross Minimax Rates for Estimating Powered Sliced Wasserstein Functionals

Authors: Binh Thuan Tran

Abstract: We study the two-sample estimation of the powered Sliced Wasserstein functional under projected $r$-th moment assumptions. Let $N=n\wedge m$, where $n$ and $m$ are two sample sizes. We establish the minimax rates in the noncritical regimes. When $p2p$ it is of order $N^{-1/2}$. In both cases, the natural plug-in estimator is minimax rate-optimal. At the endpoint $r=p$, the minimax risk is bounded away from zero, so uniformly consistent estimation is impossible. At the critical order $r=2p$, we obtain the exact worst-case rate of the plug-in estimator and a lower bound for the minimax risk, leaving a logarithmic gap between the lower and upper bounds. The exact minimax rate in this critical case remains open.

cross Credit Attribution in Timestamped Soccer Networks Using Multivariate Hawkes Processes

Authors: Phillip W. Kornberg, Kevin S. Xu

Abstract: The two main traditional player statistics used in soccer are goals scored and assists, with an assist credited when a player's pass immediately leads to a teammate scoring. Attributing credit solely to the player who makes the final pass before a goal fails to account for the contributions of multiple players in the buildup to the goal. Consequently, traditional statistics provide only a limited view of player involvement. In this paper, we use multivariate Hawkes processes (MHPs) to model timestamped sequences of passes and shots, which form continuous-time networks. MHPs provide a branching mechanism where an event (a pass or shot) can be generated as a child of a previous event, which allows us to quantify each pass's contribution to a subsequent pass or shot. This approach distributes credit across multiple passes within an attacking sequence, making it applicable across a wide range of situations. Building on this framework, we propose expected goal involvements (xGI), a metric that measures a player's involvement in creating goal scoring opportunities based on their passes. This metric identifies players who play key roles in creating goal scoring opportunities even if they do not make the final pass, offering a more comprehensive measure of attacking involvement and player performance.

cross Polyconvex neural-network surrogates with learned paired directional features for anisotropic hyperelastic homogenization

Authors: S. Ares de Parga, A. Cornejo, J. A. Hern\'andez, R. Rossi

Abstract: Approximating the effective anisotropic hyperelastic response of a representative volume element (RVE) requires constitutive expressiveness, mechanical consistency, and computational efficiency. This work develops a polyconvex physics-augmented neural network (PANN) construction based on paired directional features whose orientations, stretch powers, and determinant exponents adapt to the RVE response. The features are composed with convex, componentwise nondecreasing cores, realized as an input-convex neural network (ICNN) or an input-convex Kolmogorov-Arnold network (ICKAN). The complete energies satisfy objectivity, an exactly stress-free reference, two-dimensional polyconvexity, and prescribed growth conditions by construction, and nonnegative energy is certified separately for the selected models. On a multicavity and a single-cavity periodic cell, these energies are compared with an Unconstrained energy, trained identically without the curvature and growth constraints, and with three hyperreduced projection-based reduced-order models (HPROMs), which serve as physics-based references. On the first cell, learning the features lowers the stress error sevenfold, and six features suffice on both. The constrained energies remain below one percent in stress error and in the displacement error of a tensile-coupon FE$^2$ application with a full-order reference, whereas the Unconstrained energy and the HPROMs are more accurate inside the data. Beyond the data, however, the Unconstrained energy loses rank-one convexity, and under confined compression it stops below half of the 500 MPa that the constrained energies reach. These results quantify what the guarantees cost in accuracy and what they secure beyond the data.

cross Trainable Quantum-Native Language Models: Dissipative Memory, Capacity Limits, and Hardware Validation

Authors: Deniz Yunus G\"o\u{g}\"u\c{s}

Abstract: We study byte-level autoregressive language models whose hidden state is a small quantum register, whose transitions are learned token-conditioned quantum channels, and whose next-byte probabilities come directly from Born-rule measurements without a learned classical vocabulary projection or softmax. In the fixed-width recurrent setting, unitary dynamics cannot contract or reset stored distinctions; experimentally, unitary recurrence loses to its retrained one-token control in every tested variant. Introducing learned dissipation, then token-conditioned dissipative gating, yields useful memory beyond one token. This is consistent with, but does not establish, a capacity explanation for the short effective memory horizon. As a secondary result, one-qubit models reach lower stationary memory entropy than a heuristically optimized classical predictive frontier at matched error in 9/9 seeds. On IBM Heron hardware, computational-basis memory-register distributions show signal against a uniform null in 12/12 circuits across prefixes of up to 12 tokens, next-bit probabilities pass pre-registered accuracy bars at four-token prefixes, and bytes are generated autoregressively by sampling physically measured bit probabilities. cross VEPA: Verified Evolving Formal Policy for Lifelong LLM Agent Guardrails

Authors: Duy Nguyen, Lesly Miculicich, Bhavana Dalvi Mishra, Shuo Li, Krishnamurthy Dj Dvijotham, Mohit Bansal, Tomas Pfister, Long T. Le

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly operate over sensitive resources, such as private documents and mutable system states, which can create serious safety and security risks. Runtime guardrails should therefore make inspectable, reproducible, and deterministic allow or refuse decisions before potentially irreversible agent actions are executed. However, safety and security requirements are rarely complete before deployment, so guardrails need to keep adapting as new risks emerge. Existing adaptive guardrails learn from deployment experience, but they usually apply safety policies through LLM inference, which can lead to inconsistent decisions and ambiguous policy interpretations. Formal guardrails, on the other hand, enforce policies deterministically, but they usually rely on pre-deployment or task-specific policies and have no mechanism to adapt to newly observed risks. We introduce VEPA, a framework for the verified evolution of formal agent guardrails. VEPA represents the guardrail as a formal policy of executable symbolic rules over typed agent events. During deployment, we use an LLM guided by deployment feedback to propose policy patches that fix newly observed failures while preserving previously correct behavior. Then, a deterministic verifier only accepts policies that are structurally valid and conflict-free. Finally, an evidence-aware authority layer activates a verified rule only when it has enough supporting evidence, so uncertain updates cannot directly affect guardrail decisions. Across ASSEBench-Safety, ASSEBench-Security, and ATBench, VEPA consistently outperforms baselines while expanding the scope of deterministic formal-policy enforcement. These gains also hold under more challenging deployment conditions, including mixed streams, corrupted feedback, and longer horizons.

cross Scalable Learning of Determinantal Point Processes with Kernel-Structured Interactions

Authors: Takahiro Kawashima, Hideitsu Hino

Abstract: Determinantal point processes (DPPs) on a finite set are probability models that exhibit repulsion where inter-item similarities are encoded by a positive definite kernel matrix. Scaling DPP learning to large datasets typically requires imposing structural constraints on the kernel matrix, such as a low-rank-plus-diagonal form. However, structural assumptions introduce a severe trade-off between expressive power and computational scalability. Our study addresses this challenge by (a) modeling the full-rank interactions via a nonlinear kernel function and latent features, (b) designing a surrogate function for the maximum likelihood estimator, and (c) adopting stochastic optimization using random features of the nonlinear kernel. Numerical experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that our method significantly improves this trade-off, achieving better generalization performance with remarkable parameter efficiency.

cross COFFEQA: Counterfactual-Guided Few-Shot Distillation for LLM Question Answering

Authors: Kavinda Kehelella, Sanghamitra Dutta

Abstract: Knowledge distillation aims to train a smaller student model to mimic a larger teacher model for resource-constrained environments. However, the process of distillation is typically data-intensive and computationally expensive, particularly for generative tasks. Few-shot distillation offers an alternative by transferring teacher knowledge using limited training samples. However, we observe that few-shot distillation performance is heavily dependent on the choice of these training samples. In fact, we demonstrate that using more uncertain samples near the teacher's decision boundary can significantly deteriorate distillation performance. Motivated by this observation, we propose Counterfactual-guided Few-shot Distillation in Open-book Question Answering (COFFEQA), a novel strategy that aptly uses counterfactuals to provide additional supervision around challenging factual samples. In the generative QA setting, we define a counterfactual as a minimally modified context that induces the teacher to change its response to an alternative answer. We provide geometric insights on how factual-counterfactual pairs can constrain the deviation between the teacher's and student's decision boundaries. We further introduce a new metric, Context Following Difficulty (CFD) that further validates counterfactuals prior to distillation based on agreement with the intended alternative answer and dependence on the modified context. COFFEQA uses CFD-validated factual-counterfactual pairs for few-shot distillation. Experiments across five in-domain and three out-of-domain QA benchmarks using T5 and Qwen2.5 families show that COFFEQA outperforms factual-only distillation baselines under the same few-shot budget. For instance, on TriviaQA, COFFEQA improves over the factual-only baseline by 8.77 average EM points and 9.98 average F1 points under the T5 setting with 32 few-shot pairs.

cross Two-way Guidance: Simultaneous Learning of Summary Statistics for Generative Bayes

Authors: Percy S. Zhai, Max Pachman, Peverel D. Shipley, Jungeum Kim

Abstract: Bayesian simulation-based inference (SBI) can struggle to recover increasingly concentrated posteriors as observation size grows. We consider learning sufficient summary statistics jointly with the posterior as a general principle for scalable inference in various SBI methods. A common measure taken in SBI is dimension reduction through predetermined summaries, which may partially discard posterior information. Our approach instead imposes structure on how the posterior learner depends on a learned summary (e.g., affinity), and learns the summary simultaneously with the posterior. We identify an important mechanism named two-way guidance: under a structural restriction on the summary, the posterior sufficiency of the learned summary simplifies posterior learning, whereas a suitable posterior objective guides the learned summary towards posterior sufficiency. This mechanism explains a counterintuitive finding that the structural restriction on the summary statistics improves posterior learning, rather than limit its performance. We establish theoretical guarantees on both posterior estimation error and the learned summary's departure from sufficiency, thus identifying conditions on the simulation budget for recovering increasingly concentrated posteriors as observation size grows.

Abstract: This work studies an entropy-based estimator of Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence constructed from normalized importance weights. cross When Solvers Get Moody: Quantifying and Predicting Performance Variability in Mixed Integer Programming

Authors: Mourchid Adegbindin, Brenda Dietrich, Jamol Pender, Andrea Lodi

Abstract: Mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers are widely used, yet their performance can vary substantially even under seemingly neutral perturbations. Such variability complicates benchmarking, and the interpretation of computational studies. We introduce the Weighted Variability Score (WVS), a robust multi-component metric that aggregates dispersion in normalized runtime, final optimality gap, LP iterations, and branch-and-bound nodes across repeated runs. cross Generalized Determinantal Point Processes for Attraction and Repulsion with Distinct Interaction Kernels

Authors: Hideaki Ishibashi, Takahiro Kawashima, Hideitsu Hino

Abstract: Determinantal point processes (DPPs) model subset selection probability with repulsion, but many applications also exhibit attraction arising from pairwise relations or shared latent structures. Nonsymmetric DPPs (NDPPs) capture both effects, but their kernels are difficult to interpret and specify. We propose the determinant-ratio point process (DRPP), which models repulsion and attraction via a ratio of the determinants of two symmetric positive-definite kernels. This formulation preserves the feature-based interpretation of standard DPPs while allowing the two effects to be specified separately. Furthermore, we introduce an auxiliary-variable representation that enables efficient MCMC sampling and yields tractable expectation formulas for key statistics including the normalizing constant, which together facilitate maximum-likelihood learning. We theoretically characterize the interaction structures well suited to DRPPs: for clustered attraction with $C$ latent groups, DRPPs exactly realize the target pairwise interactions with an attraction rank $C$, whereas NDPPs can require a rank that grows with the number of items given the same repulsion component. Conversely, nontransitive pair-specific attraction may require compensation through the DRPP repulsion kernel. Experiments support these predictions, showing that DRPPs fit transitive attraction with substantially fewer parameters than NDPPs.

cross Rethinking the Roles of Diffusion and Decoding: Predictive-State Flows for Parallel Language Generation

Authors: Jinjin He, Xinsong Feng, Lucheng Fu, Sinan Wang, Bo Zhu, Jiayun Wang

Abstract: In continuous diffusion language models (DLMs), latents generated by the diffusion prior are mapped almost deterministically to tokens, either by a decoder or through a token embedding matrix. Unlike in image generation, where visual details can vary continuously, the diffusion prior must capture fine-grained discrete token choices in continuous space, which can make the joint latent distribution difficult to model. We argue that generative roles should instead be divided: the diffusion prior should generate high-level predictive information, while the decoder should resolve the remaining token-level choices. Based on this view, we introduce Predictive-State Flows (PSF), which partitions a sequence into blocks and associates each block with a predictive state computed solely from the preceding context. A rectified-flow prior generates the sequence of predictive states, after which a block-causal decoder generates all blocks in parallel, autoregressively only within each block. This design reduces diffusion from L token-level states to K << L predictive states and reduces token-level sequential depth to L/K. On unconditional OpenWebText generation, a 197M-parameter model with K=8 achieves a generative perplexity of 22.8 at entropy 5.16 using 0.31 TFLOPs per 1024-token document, versus 24.0 and 7.2 TFLOPs for ELF-B with 32 sampling steps. The corresponding measured operating points yield MAUVE@512 of 0.93 and 0.73, respectively. Controlled comparisons further show that content states, which additionally encode their own blocks, yield 8-16% higher generative perplexity than predictive states, and that replacing sampled predictive-state trajectories with a shared mean lowers MAUVE@512 by 0.11. These results suggest that latent design for continuous DLMs should consider not only how compactly text is represented, but also how generative roles are divided between the prior and the decoder.

cross Beyond Second-Order Information: Paid Logistic Regression with Noisy Features

Abstract: We consider online binary prediction with paid stochastic features, where the learner can spend resources to obtain better observations of the underlying features. cross From Imbalance to Evidence: Deep Nonparametric Testing for Complex Data under Severe Sample Imbalance

Authors: Guanhao Zhou, Xiufan Yu

Abstract: Comparing distributions under severe sample imbalance requires effectively using abundant majority observations while retaining sensitivity to differences involving the smaller sample. This challenge is particularly relevant for complex, non-Euclidean data, such as graphs, whose structural characteristics call for flexible testing procedures. Classifier two-sample tests (C2STs) provide such flexibility, but existing procedures are primarily designed for balanced or comparably sized samples. In this paper, we study C2STs in a highly imbalanced regime, and show that severe sample imbalance may substantially degrade testing performance. Theoretically, we establish rigorous guarantees on asymptotic size and characterize conditions for power consistency, allowing the imbalance ratio to diverge. We demonstrate the empirical gains using simulations and an application to blockchain forensics. The results reveal that the majority-sample surplus, when properly leveraged, can be organized into useful statistical evidence, rather than an obstacle, for distributional comparisons with complex data.

Minions enables teams to leverage existing hardware without requiring additional GPU procurement. The system provides flexible GPU inference task execution, fault tolerance, and efficient resource utilization. Our experience shows that idle macOS devices can be transformed into a practical distributed GPU inference platform in environments where centralized GPU resources are constrained.

cross NucleoNet: A Nuclear-Aware Determinant Ansatz for Multi-Geometry Neural Variational Monte Carlo

Authors: Qiao Gao, Yang Huang, Enze Hou, Yuzhi Liu, Jingrun Chen, Han Wang

Abstract: Neural-network variational Monte Carlo provides an accurate route to solving the electronic Schr\"odinger equation, but many neural wavefunctions are optimized for a single nuclear geometry, requiring separate calculations to map out a potential energy surface. We introduce NucleoNet, a nuclear-aware determinant ansatz in which electrons and nuclei form a unified particle set propagated through a shared electron--nucleus backbone that jointly evolves one-particle and two-particle representations. Nuclei carry learned representations that evolve alongside the electronic representations and condition orbital construction, while antisymmetry is imposed only by electronic determinants. On equilibrium small-molecule benchmarks, NucleoNet achieves accuracy comparable to established neural-wavefunction baselines with substantially fewer parameters. For each molecular system, a single model optimized jointly across geometries describes the N$_2$ dissociation curve, Li displacement in LiCN, and the ethane and formamide minimum-energy paths for bond dissociation. Sparse-geometry training generalizes to unseen geometries for N$_2$ and LiCN without further optimization, and N$_2$ ablations show that nuclear streams substantially improve this interpolation. These results show that a compact wavefunction with jointly evolved nuclear representations can describe the geometries of a molecular system with a single set of parameters.

cross Scan-Parallel Variational Inference for event-driven Latent Jump Models

Authors: Vansh Bansal, Min Chen, James G. Scott

Abstract: Continuous-time latent state-space models are widely used for irregularly observed time series, typically representing uncertainty through a random initial state followed by smooth ordinary differential equation (ODE) dynamics, or Brownian perturbations accumulated by a stochastic differential equation (SDE). While these constructions are natural for smooth or diffusive systems, they do not directly encode a finite set of unobserved shocks whose timing, magnitude, and effect on memory are themselves uncertain. We instead introduce latent jump models (LJMs): the latent state follows closed-form deterministic dynamics between Poisson-driven event times and undergoes a learned instantaneous jump at each event, with state-dependent intensity and mark distributions. This defines a time-homogeneous Markov process whose jumps are intrinsic state transitions rather than numerical integration steps. For training, we derive a tractable event-augmented path-space evidence lower bound (ELBO) and introduce Scan-Parallel Variational Inference (SPVI): an observation-conditioned variational posterior samples the complete latent event configuration before the state recurrence is evaluated using triangular affine scans. This enables fast and scalable training for LJMs in logarithmic temporal depth. Across synthetic stochastic systems and real irregular time series, LJMs remain competitive with continuous-time latent baselines in forecast accuracy, often with fewer latent updates than numerical solver evaluations, while training one to two orders of magnitude faster than the sequential baselines.

cross Forecasting Corpus-Sampling Disagreement in Masked Text Reconstruction

Authors: Hare Krishna

Abstract: If a second text corpus were collected from the same population, how much would a predictor's outputs change? We study this question for masked-text reconstruction using a smoothed predictor built from nearby token-pair counts and evaluated over the full 50,257-token GPT-2 vocabulary. Treating each source document as one observation, we derive a first-order formula for the expected disagreement between predictors fitted to two independent corpora. The formula accounts for variable document length, random context counts, and correlations across token offsets, and it can be evaluated from a separate reference corpus without access to either target corpus. On TinyStories, WikiText-103, and CNN/DailyMail, growing each corpus from 512 to 2,048 chunks of 64 tokens lowers disagreement by only 5--40\%, far short of the 75\% implied by $1/n$ scaling. The forecast tracks this slow decline, which we trace to smoothing of rarely seen contexts. Forecast accuracy depends less on how these correlations are modeled than on the size of the reference corpus: small references systematically underpredict disagreement. A source-split jackknife that uses only reference data reduces this calibration error by 57--59\%. After this correction, a simpler smoothed-multinomial approximation is most accurate when the reference corpus is smaller than the target corpus, and a nonlinear source bootstrap is most accurate when it is larger. Finally, small masked diffusion language models trained on the same corpora show that the count-based forecasts partly identify which inputs are most sensitive to the corpus, but miss the neural models' much faster decline in disagreement.

cross What Should SFT Learn? Conditional Information Gain for Generalizable Post-Training

Authors: Yuxuan Gu, Kequan Niu, Xiaocheng Feng, Yun Li, Yiqiao Yang, Bing Qin

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) remains a widely used approach for post-training large language models, because fixed demonstrations can directly introduce desired reasoning strategies and behavioral patterns. Yet forcing a model to imitate externally provided trajectories may also interfere with behaviors already supported by pretraining, limiting generalization beyond the training distribution. Recent dynamic SFT methods mitigate this issue by reweighting token-level supervision according to model-side signals such as predictive probability, prior support, or distributional compatibility. However, model compatibility is not equivalent to learning value: a token that is weakly supported by the current model may either be an undesirable mismatch or precisely the task-specific decision that the demonstration is intended to teach. We propose CIG-SFT, an information-theoretic framework that estimates the task-conditioned information carried by each demonstrated token. Under teacher forcing, we compare the token likelihood from a frozen reference model with and without access to the task input while keeping the gold solution prefix fixed. The resulting conditional information gain quantifies how much the task itself reduces uncertainty about the demonstrated decision. We then convert this information into a KL-regularized projection of the current policy and derive an equivalent scalar-weighted SFT objective whose logit gradient exactly matches that of the projected target. Across mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, CIG-SFT consistently outperforms standard SFT and recent adaptive fine-tuning baselines, with particularly strong improvements in generalization to challenging evaluation distributions, while requiring neither additional training data nor online rollouts.

cross Scaling Empowered Agents

Authors: Catherine Ji, Vivek Myers, Joshua Lin, Alexandra Wingate, Sergey Levine, Benjamin Eysenbach

Abstract: Empowerment measures the capacity of an agent to actively control its environment. In this work, we explain the connection between skill learning and empowerment. While skill learning is not empowerment, we introduce a simple scalable recipe for empowerment maximization: empowerment corresponds to the number of executable skills from a state, so maximizing empowerment corresponds to reaching states that are good starting points for many skills. Our recipe applies to arbitrary underlying skill learning methods. Whereas most prior work on empowerment looks at tabular settings, this recipe enables us to study empowerment at a scale where we can realize theoretically-predicted but empirically-elusive properties of empowerment (e.g., gathering resources, environment modification, survival). Experiments show that empowerment maximization unlocks interesting emergent properties, such as building a house in Crafter. Website and code at \href{https://empowerment-geometry.github.io/scaling-empowerment/}{this https url}.

cross Space Over Statistic, Retraining Over Ranking: Auditing Training Labels Under Label-Only Backdoors

Authors: Arnab Dey, Mohammad Mahfuzul Islam

Abstract: Some backdoor attacks never touch an image. FLIP only changes labels: on CIFAR-10, flipping 3% of them makes a ResNet-32 send 99.7% of triggered source-class images to the attacker's class. The usual defenses look for the poison inside a model trained on those same labels. So when they struggle, is the problem the test they run, or the space they run it in? We answer with a controlled swap: we keep the test, the labels and the metric fixed, change only the feature space, and judge each defense by retraining the victim on what it keeps. The space decides. On dog$\to$cat the same nearest-neighbor check scores AUROC 0.546 on the poisoned model's features, 0.967 on frozen DINOv2 features that never saw a label, and 0.995 on the same network trained on clean labels, an oracle control rather than a defense. We reproduce SPECTRE, the defense FLIP's authors recommend, to 90.94 / 0.97 against its published 91.47 / 1.7, then price it: on truck$\to$deer it deletes 20% of every class, while a prototype audit on the frozen features deletes 6% for higher clean accuracy and lower attack success. On three harder pairs that audit leaves about a quarter of the attack standing. A count read off encoder geometry and clean labels, before any attack is run, separates the pair where the audit removes the attack from those where it only partly does, though on a single zero-count pair. Three results warn against the easy story. A better detection score does not mean a safer model: on two of four pairs Confident Learning scores the higher AUROC, and on dog$\to$cat leaves 2.6 times the attack success. The prototype family, our best static performer, is the first an adaptive attacker slips past. And past a point, catching more poisons stops reducing attack success. Check labels in a space that never learned them, and judge a defense by the retrained model, not by its detection score.

cross CoBe: A Benchmark For Conversational Counterfactual Text Editing

Authors: Arvind Raghavan, Inwoo Hwang, Shreyas Havaldar, Kai-Zhan Lee, Aurghya Maiti, Yushu Pan, Jeffrey Wu, Mingxuan Li, Elias Bareinboim

Abstract: Counterfactual text editing is the ability to revise a given text in light of a hypothetical change while preserving facts not causally affected by that change. When asked what would have happened if a doctor had not recommended surgery, the model output should retain the patient's medical history and modify only the consequences of the decision. This requires causal understanding: a model must determine, without being explicitly instructed to, what events are affected by the hypothetical change and what should remain fixed. This skill is central to human reasoning, especially in safety-critical domains like healthcare or law. By contrast, the task of associational text generation merely generates text statistically associated with the altered condition. In the medical example, an associational response might make the patient's symptoms less severe because milder cases correlate with no surgery. Such responses produce a plausible scenario, but not the counterfactual version of the given one. Prior counterfactual-text datasets do not clearly separate these modes of reasoning. We introduce the Counterfactual Benchmark (CoBe), a testbed for models' zero-shot ability to counterfactually edit a given textual scenario when prompted to do so in a conversationally natural way. CoBe contains 2000 questions spanning healthcare, science and engineering, business, and everyday scenarios. We test a suite of frontier and smaller models, with frontier systems achieving an average score of $58.05\%$. Our findings indicate that models frequently default to associational generation rather than preserving the causal structure of the original scenario. Moreover, performance does not scale consistently with either model size or output token length, suggesting that the failure may reflect model training objectives rather than insufficient model capacity.

cross The Limits of Query Monitoring against Distributed Model Extraction

Authors: Snigdha Chandan Khilar

Abstract: Model providers now face distillation campaigns run through thousands of accounts, each of which looks like an ordinary user. When network and payment metadata cannot link those accounts, the only evidence left is what they ask. We study what a provider can and cannot learn from those queries alone, using one million real chat turns for development and 17 later weeks that no design choice touched for a single frozen evaluation. The answer is set less by anything specific to extraction than by benign coordination: a campaign is visible only if it is more distinctive than the most distinctive group of ordinary users in the same week. We make this precise, show that a small but positive difference between campaign and users buys a detection time rather than detectability, and identify the campaign that is hardest to detect. Empirically, a calibrated monitor catches naive distributed extraction that per-account monitors miss (0.86-1.00 of weeks), and one variant - selecting accounts that move together after discounting how topical each one is - still catches campaigns padded with natural text (0.50-0.96 of weeks) where the other two spectral detectors catch none. Distilling each campaign into a small student shows that, in our testbed, campaigns that evaded every detector kept at most 42% of the capability of a detectable one. The limits are equally concrete: a campaign that ramps up slowly poisons every detector's calibration, benign evaluation pipelines look exactly like distillation, we found no deployable detector at colluder shares of 2.5% or below, and campaigns that relay real users' requests may be invisible to any query-only monitor.

cross Does Location Matter? A Black-Box Causal Audit Reveals Model-Dependent Audio Grounding in LALM Hallucination

Authors: Mohammad Khalooei, Mohammad Sabokrou

Abstract: Large audio-language models (LALMs) frequently exhibit object hallucination, affirming absent sounds. cross Why Heavy-Tailed Weight Spectra Predict Model Quality

Authors: Joseph Wilson, Chris van der Heide, Liam Hodgkinson, Zhichao Wang, Fred Roosta, Michael W. Mahoney

Abstract: A solid understanding of the predictive success of deep neural networks (DNNs) remains elusive. For example, while many data-dependent metrics exist that seek to predict model quality of DNNs, these metrics may be expensive to compute for large datasets and/or may be impossible to compute when training datasets are not released. A practical and effective predictor of model quality involves analyzing DNN weight matrices, as prior work has established that heavy-tailed spectral distributions in trained weight matrices can be strongly predictive of model quality. However, such metrics lack a rigorous statistical justification. In this work, we aim to understand why these metrics perform well, from a statistical perspective. To do so, we employ a probabilistic framework to derive the marginal likelihood for a DNN, evaluated using only the final layer weights. Across large-scale convolutional DNNs and LLMs, we find that this marginal likelihood predicts model quality. To understand the role heavy-tailed spectra play in predictive performance, we derive the limiting log-marginal likelihood for spectra that follow the (heavy-tailed) High-Temperature Marchenko-Pastur (HTMP) distribution. We show that this limiting quantity is convex in a heaviness parameter, and we derive an optimal heaviness that empirically predicts over-fitting for large-scale DNNs.

cross Fast Gradient-Based Approximation of Continuous Dynamic Time Warping

Authors: Pierre Houedry, Romain Tavenard, Nicolas Courty, Romaric Gaudel

Abstract: Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) is a common choice for measuring the discrepancy between time series. It proceeds by finding an optimal warping between timestamps through dynamic programming, which can be slow and is not differentiable. Consequently, several variants have been proposed. Among them, Soft-DTW proposes a smooth formulation of DTW, amenable to gradient computation, while Continuous-DTW (CDTW) introduces a continuous formulation through parametrizable warping functions. Yet, computational costs remain high for both methods and impractical for large datasets in modern machine learning pipelines. This paper revisits CDTW in several ways. After establishing its differentiability under mild conditions, we introduce FastCDTW, which is an efficient approximate solver with two different computational strategies: an exact closed-form scheme for piecewise-linear warping functions, and an unbiased Monte Carlo estimator for smooth ones, such as implicit neural networks. Both strategies provide gradients with respect to the warping function and the input time series, and the resulting algorithm is GPU-friendly. We demonstrate its benefits through its integration into more complex learning problems, including time series averaging, classification and deep domain adaptation.

cross Looking Ahead and Back: Anticipatory Generative Modeling for Road-Network Trajectories

Authors: Zhenhao Huang, Li Sun, Philip S. Yu

Abstract: Trajectory generation underpins urban applications such as traffic management and urban planning. Within road networks, vehicle movement along road segments is largely deterministic, whereas transitions at intersections are critical--multiple outgoing paths may appear equally plausible, yet they can lead to substantially different downstream routes. Existing generative methods such as diffusion models simulate location sequences and fail short of next transition selection, even producing trajectories that drift off the road network. To fill this gap, we advocate a fundamental shift: moving beyond location simulation toward behavior modeling on the road network. In this paper, we revisit trajectory generation through the lens of generative route planning, and introduce a new graph-constrained generative paradigm, Anticipatory Generative Modeling, which resolves next transition selection by incorporating the Anticipation of how the future route may unfold toward the destination. Concretely, we propose a novel generative model named Foresight that formulates a probabilistic model for an "anticipate--select--update--re-anticipate" loop that enables longer-term foresight while continuing sequential generation along the historical trajectory. Authors: Jinyuan Xu, Shitong Shao, Lichen Bai, Zikai Zhou, Guoxi Huang, Nantheera Anantrasirichai, Zeke Xie

Authors: Fabian Drexel, Bernhard Kainz, Jakob N. Kather, Daniel Rueckert, Lisa C. Adams, Keno K. Bressem, Cosmin I. Bercea

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn by predicting teacher representations of masked regions from visible context. Point prediction summarizes the conditional target distribution and cannot reproduce variation that the context does not resolve. We call this constraint predictability bias. In computed tomography (CT), recurrent anatomy is predictable from context, whereas the sporadic, localized pathology that determines clinical interpretation often is not. Replacing visible matched-control representations increases surrounding-target prediction loss more than fully replacing pathology representations across four pathology categories. Downstream, removing all final-layer pathology tokens reduces the decision margin by at most 0.45% in four of seven scan-level probes. Together, these results show that contextual predictability is not a sufficient proxy for clinical relevance in medical representation learning. cross CV-OPD: Contrastive Vision On-policy Distillation

Authors: Haowei Wang, Hao Lin, Qi Zhu, Jiayi Ji, Deyi Ji, Liujuan Cao, Rongrong Ji

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) reveals versatility and convenience during VLM post-training, providing dense supervision for student from teacher. However, we observed an anomaly that the distilled VLM could still answer the questions accurately even when the related visual regions were masked or replaced with noise. Building on this observation, our quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that OPD, which uses the answer as a hint, has failed to teach students to genuinely seek out and rely on visual evidence. Our theoretical analysis suggests that this failure arises from a shortcut enabled by the lack of visual constraints during distillation. To address the issue, we introduce Contrastive Vision On-policy Distillation (CV-OPD), which compares teacher distributions induced by positive and negative crops while conditioning on the same student prefix and augments optimization with a margin-based contrastive constraint, which is modulated with discrepancy gating across token and sample level. Specifically, CV-OPD first introduces a negative teacher unrelated to the answer and designs a Contrastive Divergence Loss (CDL) that constrains the feature distribution of student by contrasting the positive and negative teachers, correcting how the student exploits visual regions. The CV-OPD introduces a Discrepancy Aware Gate (DAG) that uses token-level and sample-level discrepancies to select visually relevant tokens for optimization, directing the student's attention toward visual content. Extensive experiments across a broad range of fine-grained visual benchmarks demonstrate that CV-OPD substantially alleviates shortcut and consistently improves the visual reasoning capabilities of existing VLMs.

Abstract: Prompt optimizers rewrite a prompt to raise one target metric, but prompts also state requirements the metric does not score, such as an output format, citations, or abstention. cross Taking Tokenization off the Host for Agentic LLM Serving

Authors: Mohammad Siavashi, Pascal Schwinte, Ali Banaei, Gerald Q. Maguire Jr., Marco Chiesa, Dejan Kosti\'c

Abstract: Agentic workloads resend long contexts that barely change between calls. Prefix caching now skips most of their prefill, yet every request must still be tokenized in full before the cache can even be consulted. Tokenization, a CPU stage long treated as negligible, becomes the largest part of time-to-first-token (TTFT), and a frontend that tokenizes for a whole GPU fleet limits how many GPUs it can feed. On TokTier's own single-request benchmark, it is 2.6-3.9x faster than gigatoken and up to 18.1x faster than TokTier. In vLLM with a cached prefix, it lowers mean TTFT by 2.1-2.4x. The largest gain is capacity. Within a 2 s P99 target, one Dynamo frontend with GraphTok sustains 518 requests/s of 121k-token traffic, the busiest-hour load of 56 GPUs, against 296 with gigatoken and 18.5 with the default tokenizer. Across 121k to one million tokens, this is 1.75-2.5x the load gigatoken sustains, with a P99 up to 64x lower where gigatoken's tail breaks.

cross Masked Diffusion Language Agents for Tool-Integrated Chemical Reasoning

Authors: Mengdi Liu, Chenghao Jia, Hong Chang, Shiguang Shan

Abstract: Designing tool-integrated reasoning agent systems for complex chemical tasks remains a fundamental challenge. Although recent chemical agents have demonstrated promising performance through the integration of external tools, they are still built on the autoregressive paradigm, which is inherently constrained by causal attention and left-to-right generation. As a result, they often struggle with bidirectional molecular understanding, global coordinated tool planning and multi-source evidence integration. To address this challenge, we introduce ChemDiffAgent, the first masked diffusion language agent for tool-integrated chemical reasoning. It reformulates chemical reasoning as iterative denoising over interleaved reasoning and tool-use trajectories, enabling tool-use decisions to be jointly determined under full-trajectory context and thereby better aligning the agent's decision boundary with its knowledge boundary. We further analyze how this paradigm benefits agentic reasoning through harder infilling subproblems during training and flexible arbitrary-order decoding during inference. Then we develop a two-stage post-training framework, comprising agentic supervised fine-tuning and variance-reduced preference optimization, to enhance tool-use and reasoning capabilities. We also construct a comprehensive benchmark covering six chemistry tasks across both single-turn and multi-turn tool-use scenarios. Under matched budgets and evaluation, ChemDiffAgent consistently outperforms autoregressive agents, achieving an overall improvement of approximately 20%. Notably, our 8B model achieves performance comparable to, in some cases surpassing, significantly larger frontier models, such as GPT 5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. cross EmoGavel: Grounding-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards for Multimodal Emotion Preference Learning

Authors: Zilong Huang, Kong Aik Lee, Zezhong Jin, Man-Wai Mak, Tatsuya Kawahara

Abstract: Multimodal emotion preference learning uses pairwise comparisons to identify which open-ended emotion descriptions align more closely with human judgments. However, existing methods primarily focus on the final preference decision and provide little explicit supervision for localizing and correcting erroneous content. cross AgentSWE: Can Coding Agents Build the Agent You Actually Want?

Authors: Jiahao Wang, Hongjin Qian, Yuyang Hu, Jiajun Zhang, Zheng Liu

Abstract: As the production of software passes into the hands of software itself, coding agents are increasingly commissioned to build agents fitted to their users' own workflows. This delegation carries a quiet assumption, that the delivered agent will faithfully serve the requirement it was built for, rather than merely appear to. No existing benchmark tests that assumption. We introduce AgentSWE, a benchmark for agent software engineering spanning three stages of the agent development lifecycle: Creation of a complete agent from a natural-language requirement, Editing of production agent codebases, and Optimization toward a measurable target. Each of its 25 tasks is framed as a commission rather than an exam: the builder iterates against a handful of illustrative cases, while acceptance exercises the frozen submission on held-out cases designed to span the full requirement, grading behavior against requirement-derived rubrics under hard pass/fail gates or, for Optimization, against the source benchmark's own objective. Evaluating a range of coding agents across models and harnesses, we find that none comes close to delivering what a requirement asks for: the gap between what a builder demonstrates and what it delivers is the norm, not the corner case. An accompanying empirical analysis of the failure trajectories identifies recurring failure modes, among them overfitting to the visible cases, hollow runtimes behind code that looks complete, and unsupported claims of completion, each a signal by which coding agents can learn to build the agents they are asked for.

cross Overcoming Data Imbalances with Expert Selection Biases

Authors: Ayush K. Tarun, Clara Meister, Antoine Bosselut

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models have become the standard architecture for frontier LLMs. The multiple parallel experts in each transformer block theoretically enable expert specialization according to categories such as domains, languages, or modalities during pretraining. However, when data is imbalanced across categories, minority categories can become concentrated in a small fraction of the expert pool or spread inefficiently across them, effectively limiting their available capacity. In this work, we demonstrate that category-conditioned expert-selection biases can preferentially route tokens from different categories toward specialized expert pools. By applying biases based on input category, we provide a simple way to induce soft category-aligned specialization. This intervention encourages categories toward distinct expert subsets while retaining cross-category sharing when useful. More importantly, it enables the over-allocation of expert capacity to categories that would otherwise be disadvantaged by data scarcity. We observe performance improvements across modalities, low-resource languages, and code in models up to 10.6B parameters. At our smallest and largest model scales, these gains are equivalent to allocating 18.5% and 2.7% additional baseline training tokens, respectively. Overall, our results show that expert biases can serve as a lightweight control for category-level expert allocation, making room for low-resource categories while retaining standard MoE inference.

cross Model-free Bayesian Clustering and Multimodal Posterior Summarisation

Authors: Qing Li, Sara Wade

Abstract: We develop a model-free, noise-aware Bayesian clustering framework that constructs a martingale posterior over clusterings from a predictive Bayesian perspective. This avoids explicit likelihood-prior model specification, expensive MCMC sampling, and mode-seeking variational approximations, while accommodating irregular cluster shapes and enabling parallel generation of posterior draws. To interpret the posterior over high-dimensional, noise-aware clusterings, we introduce a Wasserstein-type summarization method. Using a novel noise-aware loss, this approach compresses the large collection of posterior draws into a small set of weighted representatives, capturing distinct cluster structures and noise assignments. Further, we marginalise the uncertainty in the density level, avoiding reliance on a specific choice. We establish theoretical properties of the proposed loss and guarantees for the level-marginal posterior approximation. cross Desertification Forecasting in Semi-Arid Regions: Using Deep Learning to Probe the Observability Limits of Land Degradation Dynamics

Authors: Filipe Moura Da Silva, Cl\'aubio Landney Lima Bandeira

Abstract: Forecasting desertification has received far less attention than mapping it. Abstract: Time series causal discovery --- the process of detecting causation between time series --- has its roots in signal processing research from over a half century ago. cross Does Financial Speech Need Continual Learning Regularization? A Study of EWC and mini-KFAC on Earnings Call ASR

Authors: Thai Quoc On, Homayoon Beigi

Abstract: Earnings call transcription presents a realistic continual learning scenario where Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems are updated with new recordings but risk catastrophic forgetting of previously learned financial speech patterns. cross Revisiting Accent Classification with DistilHuBERT: Speaker-Disjoint Evaluation and Cross-Corpus Generalization

Authors: Xu He, Homayoon Beigi

Abstract: Recent accent classification systems can achieve high accuracy in standard in-corpus evaluations.
