cross Learning by Compression: Risk Estimates Attain the Law-of-Large-Numbers Rate

Authors: Simone Garatti, Marco C. Campi

Abstract: Compression schemes provide a general framework for deriving data-dependent risk bounds for learning algorithms. For learning rules based on preferent compression, existing results upper-bound the risk in terms of a sample-dependent complexity $\boldsymbol{k}$, defined as the cardinality of the compression augmented by the training examples on which the reconstructed hypothesis is inappropriate. These results, however, yield a margin of order $O(\sqrt{\ln N/N})$ above $\boldsymbol{k}/N$. In this paper, we establish that the logarithmic factor is not intrinsic. We prove that, for every fixed confidence level $1-\delta$, the risk is bounded with probability at least $1-\delta$ by $$ \frac{\boldsymbol{k}}{N} + O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right), $$ uniformly over all possible values of $\boldsymbol{k}$. Thus, risk estimates for learning algorithms based on preferent compression attain the same law-of-large-numbers rate as the empirical estimate of the probability of a fixed event. We also show that the leading term $\boldsymbol{k}/N$ is necessary and investigate its relation to the empirical risk. In particular, we identify regimes in which the empirical risk retains the $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ convergence rate, as well as regimes in which it converges more slowly or ceases to be consistent, while the complexity-based estimator preserves the $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ rate.

cross SqueezeBench: Saturation-Resilient Evaluation of Mathematical Capability

Authors: Pablo Manrique, Hai Xia, Stefan Szeider

Abstract: AI systems are making rapid progress in mathematics, but this progress is hard to measure. Benchmarks of solved problems saturate, and in benchmarks of open problems each problem can be resolved for the first time only once. We present SqueezeBench, a benchmark that is resilient to saturation: a success raises the bar instead of removing the problem. Each problem fixes an integer function $f(n)$ whose values are known only for small $n$, and the benchmark keeps a lower and an upper record on it. A record is a computable Lean function with a machine-checked proof that it bounds $f$. A submission proposes a new function and proves that it bounds $f$, that it is no worse than the current record at any $n$, and that it is strictly better at some $n$. Once accepted, it becomes the record that the next submission has to beat. The system thus chooses the bound as well as its proof, and it may improve the record at a single $n$ or its growth rate. At every $n$, the gap between the two records shows how far the certified bounds are from determining $f(n)$. Every proof is checked by two independent Lean kernels, so no referee needs to check the mathematics. The checks also reject a function that computes $f$ by exhaustive search at small $n$, and a maintainer only reads the submitter's report and looks for such a search at larger $n$. We release ten problems from extremal combinatorics with twenty proved initial records and the admission tools. As a baseline, agents based on a frontier language model, with at most one hour per record, had 82 submissions accepted and improved every record, without going beyond the published literature. Agents based on two newer models then started from these records, had 60 more submissions accepted and improved 19 of the 20 records again. For eight of the ten problems, at least one record is still asymptotically behind the best published bound.

cross Harmonic analysis and circuit approximation of Sobolev functions on quantum input spaces

Authors: Philipp Schmocker

Abstract: We derive approximation rates for Sobolev functions defined on the complex unit sphere and complex projective space, representing quantum states, and on the special and projective unitary groups, representing quantum unitary operations. By using harmonic analysis on compact groups and compact homogeneous spaces, we characterize the corresponding Peter--Weyl decompositions and Sobolev spaces via irreducible representations and the Laplace--Beltrami operator. Combining spectral truncation with finite-depth quantum-circuit approximation, invoking the Solovay--Kitaev theorem, we establish quantitative rates that separate the spectral truncation error from the circuit approximation error.

cross A Scalable Learned Iterative Solver for Nonlinear MPC via Temporal Decomposition

Authors: Lukas L\"uken, Sergio Lucia

Abstract: Nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) can be applied to large nonlinear systems with complex objectives while enforcing constraints, but the cost of solving the underlying optimization problems limits real-time application for systems with fast dynamics or many states. Neural networks can reduce this online cost by approximating the control law, but may violate constraints and typically require many precomputed optimizer solutions for training. The learning-based iterative solver for constrained optimization (LISCO) instead learns to predict primal-dual steps toward satisfaction of the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) conditions through self-supervised training without precomputed solutions, but its dense, monolithic architecture grows with the prediction horizon. We remove this horizon dependence by exploiting the temporal structure of the problem in the solver architecture, which makes the network size independent of the horizon while leaving the training loss and the solver iteration unchanged. A closed-loop resampling strategy further aligns the training distribution with closed-loop operation at no additional sampling cost. On an economic NMPC benchmark of a CSTR cascade, the proposed solver scales to larger problems than monolithic LISCO and achieves median speedups of up to a factor of four over IPOPT. In closed loop, it achieves comparable control performance to IPOPT with no observed constraint violations.

cross Asymptotic Analysis of Discrete-Time Self-Attention Dynamics in Transformers

Authors: Claudio Altafini

Abstract: In machine learning, a self-attention dynamics describes the attention mechanism of transformers, and is obtained treating the layer index of a transformer as time index. Even though naturally discrete-time, this dynamical system is most often studied in continuous-time. Scope of this paper is to provide a thorough investigation of the discrete-time version of the self-attention dynamics: a complete classification of its equilibria is given, and their stability properties are studied, in both the single-head and the multihead case. We show that in both cases the main attractor of the dynamics is a consensus equilibrium aligned with the principal eigenvector of the value matrix (in the single-head case, a corresponding matrix of weights in the multihead case), even though other locally asymptotically stable bipartite equilibria can be show to exist at least in the single-head situation. The behavior is confirmed by looking at the learned weight matrices of a real transformer.

cross S-Space: Exploring Spatial Workspace in Multimodal Language Models

Authors: Zhiyuan Shi, Xiang Li, Xingzhuo Guo, Shangchen Miao, Weiliang Chen, Qiyu Dai, Jinshan Ren, Jiangran Lyu, Fangfu Liu, Mingsheng Long, Jialong Wu

Abstract: Multimodal language models (MLLMs) exhibit promising spatial reasoning capabilities, yet the internal mechanisms supporting them remain poorly understood. We uncover S-Space, a linear representation subspace in which object-token representations encode continuous coordinates along horizontal, vertical, and distance axes. Although we identify these axes by differentiating spatial-answer scores with respect to object-token activations, S-Space is intrinsic rather than induced by specific data or prompts, remaining readable under non-spatial questions. We show that S-Space functions as an internal spatial workspace of MLLMs. Perception encodes object locations into it, forming spatial representations that can be directly read out to support accurate spatial judgments. Case studies further show that S-Space assigns locations to unseen entities and the observer, and connects physical directions with abstract linguistic meanings, suggesting both the capabilities and limitations of large-scale language training. Finally, we use S-Space as a diagnostic tool for spatial reasoning failures. Direction-word readouts reveal systematic coupling between cardinal and image-relative reference frames. More importantly, a perception-computation decomposition exposes a key bottleneck: MLLMs can form capable spatial representations, yet fall short in the spatial transformations required to reason reliably across viewpoint changes.

Abstract: When a language model is distilled from a teacher that shares its base parameters, the student can inherit behavioural traits of the teacher---including misalignment---even from data that is semantically unrelated to the trait (Cloud et al., 2026). Abstract: Pre-trained robot policies, such as Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, provide a strong foundation for generalist robotic manipulation, but adapting them to new tasks with only a few demonstrations remains challenging. cross Lower Bounds for Randomized Linear-Oracle Optimization over Strongly Convex Sets

Authors: Sebastian Pokutta

Abstract: Recent results [arXiv:2602.04378, arXiv:2602.22608] have established worst-case objective-error lower bounds of order $1/T^2$ after $T$ linear minimization oracle (LMO) calls, matching the upper bound [arXiv:1406.1305] in its dependence on $T$ and thereby resolving a long-standing open question. The second [arXiv:2602.22608] obtains lower bounds for any deterministic LMO-based first-order method via a resisting oracle that adversarially adapts to the queries of the algorithm. While our construction reuses the family of [arXiv:2602.22608], our proof characterizes the conditional distribution of the hidden permutation after adaptive queries and bounds the expected decrease of a combinatorial statistic controlling optimizer variance. We further show that the same error threshold holds with probability at least $3/4$ and yields a stopping-time tail bound.

cross CorrFlow: Exact and Permutation-Equivariant Flow Matching on the Correlation Elliptope

Authors: Gautier Marti

Abstract: Correlation matrices combine nonlinear positive-definiteness constraints with a simultaneous row-column permutation symmetry. Their generation therefore involves two distinct problems. First, every output must remain on the correlation elliptope, irrespective of how its variables are ordered. Second, the generator must learn the domain-specific distribution occupied by empirical data inside that valid space. Existing financial image generators mix these problems: they learn ordered matrix entries and repair invalid draws afterward. We introduce CorrFlow, which separates them. A generalized Fisher transform maps full-rank correlation matrices bijectively to unconstrained coordinates, guaranteeing valid outputs without projection. A graph flow-matching network learns the empirical distribution in those coordinates and is permutation equivariant: reordering the variables only reorders the output. We compare a conditional CorrGAN reimplementation, image diffusion, and CorrFlow on $80\times80$ Taiwan equity matrices using five seeds per neural method. CorrFlow produces 100% valid draws without repair and obtains feature sliced-Wasserstein distance $.712\pm.141$, versus $4.339\pm.252$ for CorrGAN and $4.610\pm.191$ for image diffusion after their invalid outputs are projected back to the correlation space. It also improves this distance over historical lookup, while lookup retains higher local coverage. These results support the combined construction under the evaluated training budget; they do not establish superiority over fully tuned image models or isolate the contribution of each component.

Abstract: Vocal intensity conversion renders a recorded utterance at a different vocal intensity, rather than simply scaling its amplitude, while preserving its content and speaker identity. cross Some remarks on the connection between generative diffusion and optimal transport

Authors: Giulio Biroli, Marc M\'ezard

Abstract: We discuss three simple connections between generative diffusion and optimal transport. First, the joint law of the data and of their Ornstein-Uhlenbeck noising is, at every time, the solution of an entropic optimal-transport problem, at a temperature set by the noise level. Backward diffusion can consequently be read as an annealing which gradually decreases the entropic regularization. Second, we study the speciation transition--how the modes of the data distribution emerge during generation--and compare how it happens in diffusion and flow matching with respect to optimal transport. Using Gaussian mixtures as high-dimensional example, we show that the dynamical instability through which diffusion and flow matching separate the modes becomes, for optimal transport, an exponentially thin boundary layer at the beginning of generation, which is rounded by entropic regularization. Third, considering a generic forward dynamics with a general confining potential, we show that the backward process driven by the score also satisfies a drift-based variational principle whose minimal cost is the entropy difference between initial and final distributions.

cross Kernel-based External Validity of Experimental Studies

Authors: Federico Matteucci, Gerhard Neumann

Abstract: The external validity of a study is the extent to which its findings generalize to unseen experimental conditions, such as datasets. We formalize the results of a study together with its research question, and define external validity as the probability that two independent and same-sized replications of the study produce results whose maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) is below a given tolerance. This approach, centered on the choice of a space of results and of an appropriate reproducing kernel for the MMD, is flexible enough to be deployed on multiple different types of results and research questions. Additionally, we establish an asymptotic power-law relation between the size of a study and the quantiles of the MMD, which we use to estimate the desired size of an externally valid study from a small-scale preliminary study. We deploy our framework to rankings with ties and discuss kernels suited to different research questions. Experiments on existing case studies and on a simulated newly designed study illustrate the applicability of the framework. We release our code as part of the genexpy Python module.

cross Electron-Density-Informed Molecular Property Prediction Using Surrogate Functionals

Authors: Manuel V. Klockow, Simon Wagner, Roman Remme, Dominic Plein, Fred A. Hamprecht

Abstract: Machine learning methods are increasingly used to predict chemical properties directly from the molecular structure to bypass the often prohibitive cost of quantum mechanical calculations. Recent work has shown that including the ground-state electron density as input to the machine learning model can improve accuracy in this setting. This work employs the recent surrogate functional framework originally developed for orbital-free density functional theory and extends it to predicting molecular properties. We show that passing the ground-state density as input to the model, while simultaneously enforcing it to be the minimum of the surrogate functional, surpasses state-of-the-art performance for six out of seven extensive properties of the QM9 dataset. Additionally, the approach shows improved generalization to larger molecules taken from the QM40 dataset. On all extensive properties of both datasets, our approach outperforms models that are informed by the ground-state density but do not enforce the surrogate functional constraints.

cross A Critical Look at Power Sampling when Test Time Sharpening LLMs

Authors: Abiel Malkani Talwar, Christopher Nemeth, Henry Moss

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved LLM reasoning, yet growing evidence suggests it mainly performs distribution sharpening: concentrating probability mass on reasoning paths the base model already produces. Recent work therefore seeks RLVR's gains without needing reward signals, by sampling from the power distribution $\pi_\alpha \propto \pi_{\text{base}}^\alpha$ via Markov chain Monte Carlo or sequential Monte Carlo. We show that these samplers fail to converge to $\pi_\alpha$ under realistic compute budgets, and that their gains are largely recreated via simple Best-of-$N$ with appropriate grading . This suggests they succeed by selecting high-probability sequences, not by approximating $\pi_\alpha$, raising a natural question: is the power distribution itself a worthwhile target? We test this hypothesis directly by fine-tuning the base model to match $\pi_\alpha$, sidestepping sampler convergence issues entirely. Despite approximating the target far more faithfully than existing samplers, this strategy yields gains that are noisy and specific to certain models and sampling tasks. Together, our results suggest that the value of power sampling lies not in faithfully targeting $\pi_\alpha$, but in the simpler goal of extracting sequences the base model already deems likely.

cross Scalable Sparse Autoregressive Variational Inference for Non-Gaussian Spatial Fields

Authors: Paul F. V. Wiemann, Matthias Katzfuss

Abstract: Latent field models are essential for spatial statistics, but the posterior distribution of the latent field, even with a Gaussian process (GP) prior, is analytically intractable whenever the likelihood is non-Gaussian. Existing approximations are either too slow or too restrictive for large non-Gaussian datasets. We present a scalable variational inference framework that mirrors the sparse nearest-neighbor autoregressive structure of the prior in the variational family, uses normalizing-flow conditionals for expressiveness, and amortizes all variational parameters via a single shared neural conditioner. Doubly stochastic optimization with reduced ancestor sets achieves polylogarithmic complexity per iteration.

cross A Multi-Agent LLM System for Automated Tuning of Specialized Neural Network Models

Authors: Ivan Moloshnikov, Dmitry Kunitsyn, Alexander Sboev, Maksim Skorokhodov, Roman Rybka, Yuanchao Liu

Abstract: Many machine learning approaches are two-level: an expert selects a class of methods, within this class the choice of key components is critical and usually manual. This paper addresses the automatic tuning of these components and proposes a framework transferable across models of different nature. The multi-agent large language model (LLM) framework has three roles---analyst, coder, and recorder~---with an experiment log and a sandboxed execution environment. Unlike systems that optimize the entire ML script or search architectures in a discrete space, the framework operates over an open but semantically narrow space: the agent generates code only for the specified components, leaving the overall network structure unchanged. The approach is validated in two directions: tuning a classification spiking neural network with correlation learning, and solving high-order differential equations with Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN). Across five tasks---spiking classification on MNIST and Digits, and boundary-value problems for the generalized nonlinear Schr\"odinger equation (GNLSE) and Kuramoto--Sivashinsky (KS) equations---the agent outperforms the baselines: classification accuracy is 13 percentage points (MNIST) and 6 percentage points (Digits) higher; the error against the analytical single-soliton solution is reduced approximately 2491-fold; the invariant-preservation error drops approximately 325-fold in the selected GNLSE run with a Gaussian perturbation and by more than 100-fold on average over repeated initializations; and the mass drift drops 14-fold for the KS problem with a Gaussian perturbation. The framework thus tunes key components on tasks of different nature without changing its internal structure.

cross Not All Dimensions Are Equal: Outlier-Aware Attention Linearization with Grouped Quadratic Kernels

Authors: Weiqi Sun, Qianyue Wu, Wenjia Wang, Tianyang Hu

Abstract: Linearizing softmax attention offers a route to efficient Transformer inference, but preserving pretrained attention remains challenging. We identify an important source of approximation error overlooked by standard linearization methods: a small number of outlier dimensions contribute disproportionately to query-key dot products and account for a large fraction of the approximation error. Motivated by this heterogeneity, we propose Outlier-Aware Attention Linearization (OAL), a dimension-aware method for constructing quadratic attention approximations. Starting from a second-order Taylor approximation, we introduce a Hadamard-product formulation that makes individual query-key contributions explicit. This formulation relaxes globally shared coefficients to support dimension-specific linear terms and cross-dimensional quadratic interactions while preserving separable evaluation. To retain this flexibility with fewer parameters, OAL groups dimensions with similar contribution scales to share coefficients, while allowing distinct treatment of the few outliers. The resulting positive quadratic kernel is calibrated against pretrained attention outputs and admits linear-time evaluation with respect to sequence length for fixed head dimensions. On Qwen2.5-0.5B, OAL reduces frozen-attention output relative MSE by approximately 8.9% compared with DiJiang. Replacing middle-layer attention modules and jointly fine-tuning the kernel coefficients with LoRA further lowers WikiText-103 perplexity from 31.71 to 27.50 compared with a learned global quadratic, while improving accuracy on PIQA, HellaSwag, and ARC-E. These results demonstrate the value of outlier-aware dimension grouping for both attention approximation and pretrained-model conversion. The code is available at https://github.com/wuqianyue09/OAL

URLs: https://github.com/wuqianyue09/OAL

cross X-AVDD: Cross-Attentive Audio-Visual Dataset Distillation

Authors: Saumyaranjan Mohanty, Aravind Reddy, Konda Reddy Mopuri

Abstract: Audio-visual dataset distillation aims to synthesize a small dataset from a large paired audio-visual dataset, while nearly preserving the training utility for neural networks. Existing approaches such as DM and AVDD face two critical limitations: 1) they are memory-intensive, making them difficult to scale to complex architectures and higher samples-per-class (SPC) settings, and 2) they often fail to preserve strong cross-modal semantic correspondence. To tackle these issues, we propose X-AVDD, a cross-attention-based distillation method that explicitly captures inter-modal relationships during synthesis. By decoupling cross-modal alignment from joint optimization, X-AVDD is substantially more memory-efficient, reducing end-to-end synthesis cost by $\sim 8\times$ GPU-hours, while producing highly aligned synthetic data. Empirically, X-AVDD significantly outperforms other audio-visual distillation methods. On VGGSound, an audio-visual dataset of short YouTube clips, X-AVDD improves top-1 test accuracy from $8.2\%\rightarrow24.0\%$ at SPC=$10$ and $9.8\%\rightarrow26.5\%$ at SPC=$20$. Furthermore, X-AVDD improves cross-architecture generalization (e.g., $25.5\%\rightarrow35.3\%$ for a ViT target on AVE dataset at SPC $=50$) and successfully scales to complex architectures such as AudioCLIP, achieving $62.3\%$ accuracy using only $5.65\%$ of the full dataset, against the full dataset accuracy of $72.4\%$ while completing in $9\times$ lower training time compared to the full dataset.

cross Adaptive DHiSparse: Speculative Decoding over a Load-Adaptive KV Hierarchy

Authors: Naji Alazhar, Wassel Alazhar

Abstract: Long-context serving runs out of KV memory before the hardware reaches its useful throughput. HiSparse stores history in host memory and fetches only the selected entries, but it uses one cache budget at every load and does not handle draft-model speculative decoding such as DSpark and DFlash2. We present Adaptive DHiSparse, which supports speculative decoding over hierarchical sparse KV and adapts residency to load. It protects the required working set, retains additional history while GPU memory is spare, and recovers the rest through exact fused retrieval. DSpark and DFlash2 keep their proposal and acceptance rules: queries share missing-row retrieval, draft KV uses a window-bounded ring, and verification staging is reused across layer groups. At 100K context and concurrency 128, GLM-5.3 with DFlash2 delivers 793 output tokens/s per decode GPU, 1.45$\times$ HiSparse and 1.27$\times$ non-speculative adaptive serving. Held to the same latency bounds as HiSparse, it sustains up to 4$\times$ the concurrency and 2.3 to 3.7$\times$ the output, depending on the per-token bound. On DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash at 500K context and concurrency 512, Adaptive DHiSparse with DSpark delivers 1.85$\times$ the output of resident serving with DSpark. In a load step, optional residency reduces host fetches from 14.2% to 1.4% of selected rows at low load and releases memory under pressure.

cross SmolWAM: A Latent World-Action Model for Robotic Manipulation on Edge Devices

Authors: Timi Owolabi, Hikmah Olawore, Frances Adelakun, Ayotomiwa Oyewumi

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) predict a robot's actions together with the observations those actions will produce. However, the strongest models rely on video diffusion transformers that are far too computationally expensive for resource-constrained edge devices. We present SmolWAM, a latent WAM that we derive from the 2B-parameter Cosmos Policy by keeping its pretrained trunk and rebuilding its inference interface: single-frame visual latents, cached text features that preserve the trunk's attention sinks, 12 of 28 transformer blocks, and 1,008 tokens from which actions and future visual latents are read out in one rectified-flow step. Trained in 3.6 GPU-hours, SmolWAM succeeds in 95.5% of 400 LIBERO episodes at 57 ms and 2.3 TFLOP per 16-action chunk with 2.4 GiB of GPU memory, 11x faster than its teacher with 18.5x fewer FLOPs and 3.2x less memory, and its world head still forecasts scene motion.

cross A Privileged Teacher That Only Scores Cannot Search: Critique-Conditioned On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning

Authors: Mayug Maniparambil, Ayush Rai, Ekansh Verma, Noel E. O'Connor, Fergal Reid

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation promises to replace the sparse outcome signal of verifiable-reward RL with dense token-level credit: a teacher conditioned on the reference solution scores the student's own rollout, and the resulting distributional shift is distilled back into the policy. We investigate whether this scoring signal actually contains corrective credit. Using gold error spans in student rollouts, we test the two signals consumed by self-distillation objectives, the full-distribution KL and the token log-ratio, for two properties: whether they localize errors and whether they push probability away from erroneous student tokens. They exhibit neither of these properties nor can they reliably distinguish correct from incorrect rollouts within the same problem. We argue that this failure is structural: locating an error in a reasoning trace is itself a search problem, while a scoring teacher is never explicitly asked to perform that search. The same model, with the same privileged information, localizes errors substantially better when allowed to generate a critique. This forms the motivation for \emph{critique-conditioned self-distillation}: first generate a critique of the current rollout, then condition the scoring teacher on that critique. The resulting signal localizes errors, is corrective within error spans, and shifts probability toward tokens that initiate backtracking such as ``Wait'', ``But'', and ``Actually''. We further show that the critique is useful both at test time, where resuming a wrong rollout with the critique in context converts math problems the model cannot solve by sampling, and in cold-start GRPO, where it provides an advantage signal on prompts for which verifiable rewards alone provide none.

cross How Well Do Prior-Data Fitted Networks Amortize Bayesian Inference?

Authors: Manuel H\"ulskamp, Julius Kobialka, Emanuel Sommer, Thomas Nagler, David R\"ugamer

Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) are trained to approximate the Bayesian posterior predictive distribution (PPD) of a synthetic prior, yet results about the approximation quality are surprisingly thin. Taking an empirical risk minimization perspective, we decompose the deployment error of PFNs into prior, context, and amortization error, and measure the latter against a reference PPD under a realistic prior. Results show that PFNs generally recover the targeted PPD very well. Nevertheless, we find that parts of the residual amortization error persist, especially in \textit{rare} settings under the prior pretraining distribution, even when scaling the model size, which manifests in disagreement between independently pretrained PFNs and leaves uncertainty about the predictor at inference time. As a remedy, we study multiple types of Bayesian model averages and find that these can consistently reduce this uncertainty and improve predictions up to full-size TabICLv2 models.

cross Shortcut Learning in Geospatial Foundation Model Embeddings for Cross-Disaster Damage Assessment

Authors: Ali Shibli, Andrea Nascetti, Yifang Ban

Abstract: Geospatial foundation models can support disaster damage assessment, but high accuracy on familiar disasters may hide shortcut learning: prediction from event or scene characteristics rather than evidence that transfers to a new disaster. We study embeddings from TerraMind, Prithvi-EO-2.0, DOFA, and DINOv3 on the multi-disaster datasets xView2 and BRIGHT. Holding out an entire disaster, instead of randomly splitting image tiles, reduces mean balanced accuracy from 0.807 to 0.638 on xView2 and from 0.818 to 0.568 on BRIGHT. Four sets of tests examine what information supports prediction: event and acquisition context, before/after image inputs, building count, and correct image pairing. Pre-event images remain predictive, and a classifier given only the annotated number of buildings reaches balanced accuracy of 0.629 and 0.653. Combining before/after features performs best on average on xView2, but does not improve over pre-only features on BRIGHT. Shuffling image pairs within the same disaster and damage class causes modest losses. Motivated by these findings, we test whether feature transformations learned only from training disasters improve generalization to unseen disasters. These transformations improve performance, with supervised contrastive learning performing best on xView2 and PCA on BRIGHT. Our results highlight the importance of evaluation on unseen domains for assessing model robustness and show that simple feature-level transformations can improve generalization. Code and experiments can be found at https://github.com/alishibli97/gfm-shortcut-learning.

URLs: https://github.com/alishibli97/gfm-shortcut-learning.

cross From Pixels to Privacy-Sensitive Features: An Information-Theoretic Framework for Reconstruction Bounds

Authors: Henry Bourne, Robert F. Allison

Abstract: What can an adversary recover from a model trained with DP-SGD? Its $(\varepsilon,\delta)$ guarantee does not answer this question directly. We derive a distortion bound that can be computed before training and applies even to an adversary who observes the parameter trajectory during training, knows every other training record and the population distribution from which the training records are drawn. The analyst may choose the representation in which error is measured, allowing the bound to target the aspects of reconstruction that are privacy-sensitive in a given application. Our information-theoretic framework separates two contributions: the information about a record revealed by training, measured through conditional mutual information, and the uncertainty already present in the data distribution, measured through source entropy. Rate-distortion theory combines them into a lower bound on the expected error of every reconstruction attack. For DP-SGD, the leakage bound depends only on the sampling rates and noise multipliers, while the source entropy is lower-bounded from a sample with finite-sample confidence. An orthogonal block construction keeps the resulting bound informative for high-dimensional images. We instantiate the framework in pixel space and in LPIPS feature space, obtaining, to our knowledge, the first lower bound on reconstruction distortion measured directly by LPIPS. On a range of image datasets the bounds establish non-trivial unavoidable expected reconstruction error within heuristically recommended privacy-budget ranges and beyond. Experiments compare the bounds with strong reconstruction attacks and identify the principal sources of the remaining slack.

cross Geometry-Aware Multi-Objective Optimization: Norm-Constrained Oracles with Entropic Weight Updates

Authors: El Mahdi Chayti, Alireza Mirrokni, Martin Jaggi

Abstract: Multiple-gradient descent is typically formulated in Euclidean geometry, whereas matrix-aware optimizers use directions induced by other norms. We analyze a single-loop multi-objective method combining a norm-constrained linear minimization oracle with entropic updates of task weights. For any finite-dimensional norm pair, we establish an $O(T^{-1/2})$ ergodic stationarity rate in the deterministic setting, deriving the required trajectory gradient bound from smoothness and lower-boundedness in the unregularized case. With stochastic gradients and a bounded-gradient assumption, one persistent blended-gradient momentum buffer yields an $O(T^{-1/4})$ rate whose leading term has no explicit dependence on the number of tasks. We also establish static regret for the inner weighting objective on arbitrary bounded gradient sequences and, with entropy regularization and a weight floor, iteration-wise inner-gap control after burn-in while retaining ergodic stationarity, conditional on a trajectory gradient bound. The optimal blended gradient can be nonunique in spectral and sign geometries, motivating a selection-independent inner gap. Our analysis extends the spectral construction studied by concurrent MOON to general norms. Experiments compare the method with existing multi-task optimizers and examine its oracle, weighting, and momentum choices.

cross Optimal Algorithms for Nonsmooth Nonconvex Stochastic Optimization with Constraints

Authors: Gon Buzaglo, Guy Kornowski

Abstract: We present new algorithms for stochastic optimization problems in which the objective is neither smooth nor convex, subject to a convex constraint set. Our algorithms return a $(\delta,\varepsilon)$-Goldstein stationary point, as recently defined for constrained problems by Liu et al. (2024), with improved complexities, and are the first to achieve optimal convergence rates for this task in several settings. In particular, we design projection-based and projection-free algorithms that achieve a $\widetilde O(\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{-3})$ rate using stochastic gradients, which we show to be optimal, and a $\widetilde O(d\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{-3})$ rate using zeroth-order queries, which is also optimal in its dependence on the dimension $d$ and improves over prior work. Notably, our lower bound is given in terms of the Frank-Wolfe gap and holds even for smooth problems, which is of independent interest. These results show that in nonconvex stochastic optimization, the convergence rate is degraded neither by the presence of constraints nor by nonsmoothness. Our analysis is based on a reduction to online linear optimization, extending a related technique from unconstrained optimization.

cross Adaptive Variance Reduction without Knowing Oracle Smoothness

Authors: Wei Jiang, Yibo Wang, Zechao Li

Abstract: Variance reduction improves the convergence rate of stochastic non-convex optimization under the average smoothness condition. Existing adaptive variance reduction methods are especially designed for average smoothness and do not adapt to standard smoothness. In this paper, we study how one algorithm can adapt to these two settings and the noiseless setting without knowing the smoothness constants or the variance bound. Given the gradient budget $T$, we develop a two-phase method. The first phase compares two independent gradients and switches to normalized momentum when their difference exceeds a threshold. This phase provides both a candidate with standard smooth and noiseless guarantees and also ensures a bounded expected function gap for the second variance reduction phase. The resulting rates are $O(T^{-1/3})$ under average smoothness, $O(T^{-1/4})$ under standard smoothness, and $O(T^{-1/2})$ without noise. When $T$ is unknown, we propose a method with two paths that attain the corresponding rates with logarithmic factors in the stochastic settings. Here, we use a variance reduction path to deal with average smoothness and employ the AdaGrad-Norm for standard smoothness and noiseless setting. Neither method restarts from the initial point or requires a growing number of oracle calls in each round.

cross Constrained Probabilistic Numerics: Gaussian Process Solvers for Linear PDEs under Linear Inequality Constraints

Authors: Boyuan Deng, Kshitiz Upadhyay, Michael Shields

Abstract: Gaussian process solvers for linear partial differential equations (PDEs) return posteriors rather than point estimates, but they condition only on equality information: the PDE residual, boundary data, and measurements. Many solutions of interest also obey inequality constraints, including nonnegativity, monotonicity, and absence of arbitrage, which equality conditioning cannot enforce. We prove that conditioning a Gaussian process prior jointly on linear equality observations and a finite-dimensional inequality event is equivalent to conditioning sequentially in terms of Borel measures on the solution space. The physics-informed posterior is therefore a valid prior for the inequality update at virtual points. The combined law is non-Gaussian yet computable through truncated-Gaussian moments, and neither the PDE conditioning nor the inequality update requires modification. We evaluated the construction on a Poisson benchmark, option-price surfaces, and air-quality fields. Under incomplete boundary data, a monotonicity constraint lowered the error of a two-dimensional Poisson reconstruction by $63\%$. On a real S&P 500 option chain thinned to $20$ retained quotes, no-arbitrage constraints reduced held-out extrapolation error by $22\%$ and cut the calendar-arbitrage rate from $10\%$ to $1\%$. On four real air-quality snapshots, nonnegativity reduced a Gaussian moment-based sub-zero-tail proxy by $74\%$ at the sparsest monitor count. Changes in mean accuracy and constraint diagnostics differed across data regimes. The probability of the noisy virtual constraint event under the equality posterior measures agreement with the specified bounds before the update. The construction turns admissibility from a post-processing step into conditioning information for probabilistic PDE solvers.

cross Diversity-Aware Ensemble of Quantum-Classical Models via Combinatorial Fusion

Authors: Jingyan Xu, Yuanhong Wu, Samuel Yen-Chi Chen, Sean Patten, Christina Schweikert, D. Frank Hsu

Abstract: Variational quantum circuits (VQCs) are highly sensitive to circuit architecture, yet most quantum machine learning pipelines select a single best design and discard the remaining candidates. This paper addresses whether the structural diversity across quantum circuit search spaces can be used to produce stronger predictions through model fusion. We apply Combinatorial Fusion Analysis (CFA), a model fusion framework that selects and combines multiple scoring systems using rank-score function (RSF) and cognitive diversity (CD), to a pool of 29 architecturally diverse classical models and quantum circuits. Using a diversity-aware model selection method, we identify subsets of maximally diverse models and combine them with both score and rank combination. Experiments on four binary classification benchmarks covering different domains demonstrate that CFA-based hybrid classical-quantum fusion consistently outperform the best individual model on every dataset considered with improvements up to 6.03% in accuracy. When comparing with relevant literatures, the CFA framework further achieves superior performance across all four datasets.

cross Dream to Remember: Continual Steering of Generalist Robot Policies with World Models

Authors: Bahey Tharwat, Iman Nematollahi, Marvin Alles, Xingyuan Zhang, Akshay L Chandra, Abhinav Valada

Abstract: Generalist robot policies are trained with imitation learning and are bounded by the quality and coverage of their demonstrations. Reinforcement learning can improve and extend them, either by fine-tuning the policy weights or by training a small module to steer the input noise of a frozen generative policy. When tasks arrive sequentially, both approaches suffer from catastrophic forgetting. Fine-tuning on a task degrades prior tasks, and steering only shifts forgetting to the shared steering module. We propose Dream to Remember (D2R), which continually steers a frozen generalist policy toward new tasks, while retaining its earlier skills. As the steering module learns a new task, a frozen pretrained world model rehearses prior tasks through imagined rollouts, anchoring the module to its earlier behavior without a replay buffer or new data. When the world model is faithful enough, D2R learns the new task entirely in imagination. Otherwise, it learns the task in the environment while using the world model only to rehearse prior tasks. On CALVIN, D2R improves eight tasks in sequence entirely in imagination, raising average success from 63.7% to 74.8%, while LoRA fine-tuning on the same sequence drops it to 11.7%. With a frozen off-the-shelf generalist policy and world model, $\pi_0$ and A2World, D2R learns unseen LIBERO-90 tasks in the environment while keeping $\pi_0$'s performance on the LIBERO suites it was fine-tuned on, even though the world model is not accurate enough for policy optimization. Finally, on a real Franka robot, D2R improves three tasks in sequence entirely in imagination while retaining the earlier improvements.

cross Finite-Sample Guarantees for Fully Discrete Conditional Diffusion Models

Authors: Qiuyun Zhu

Abstract: We establish finite-sample guarantees for conditional diffusion models learned from data and implemented with finitely many denoising steps. Our approach aligns the score-training objective with the sampling grid, allowing statistical estimation and numerical discretization errors to be analyzed together. Under a smooth conditional density model, we derive high-probability bounds on the generated distribution's error and quantify the required number of score evaluations. For every smoothness index $\beta>0$, a suitable grid yields averaged conditional Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence $\widetilde O_p(n^{-2\beta/(d_x+d_y+2\beta)})$ relative to the original target with or without early stopping. Both the full and early-stopped samplers attain average target total-variation (TV) error $\widetilde O_p(n^{-\beta/(d_x+d_y+2\beta)})$. Here $n$ is the sample size, $d_x,d_y$ are the data and conditioning dimensions, and errors are averaged over the conditioning variable. The total-variation bound matches the rate established in existing conditional distribution-estimation theory, up to logarithmic factors.

cross Hitting-Time Bounds for Asynchronous Q-Learning

Authors: Yang Peng

Abstract: We study the last iterate of standard asynchronous Q-learning from a single trajectory of a finite Markov decision process. The trajectory follows a fixed behavior policy whose state--action chain is irreducible and may be periodic. For discount factor $\gamma$, write $H=(1-\gamma)^{-1}$, and let $\mu_{\min}$ be the minimum stationary state--action probability and $\mathfrak h_\star$ the largest expected hitting time of the reverse state--action chain. We prove that zero-initialized Q-learning with a constant step size achieves sup-norm error at most $\varepsilon$ with high probability using $\widetilde{O}(H^4/(\mu_{\min}\varepsilon^2)+\mathfrak h_\star)$ transitions, for $0<\varepsilon\le1$. The statistical term retains the quartic effective-horizon dependence of standard Q-learning, while initialization cost is additive in $\mathfrak h_\star$. For aperiodic behavior chains, a comparison with mixing time yields a transient term $\widetilde{O}(t_{\mathrm{mix}}/\mu_{\min})$. An empirical state--action count rule selects a constant step size for a subsequent training run and attains the same rate without prior knowledge of model parameters, target accuracy, or confidence level. The output is the last training iterate, with both stages included in the sample count. The proof uses two deterministic Bellman envelopes to separate statistical error from fixed-policy initialization error and adapts reverse-time hitting-cost compensation from TD learning to state--action propagation. An exploration lower bound holds for arbitrary predictable step sizes. Together with the existing statistical lower bound and its extension to pilot-selected constant steps, it yields matching mean-square rates up to logarithmic factors on the stated large-sample parameter classes.

cross DyssomniaNN: Multi-Scale Spiking Neural Modeling of Human Sleep Disorders and Perceived Restedness

Authors: Daniel Szelogowski

Abstract: Clinical sleep medicine repeatedly documents profound divergences between objective sleep duration and subjective daytime impairment, notably exemplified by sleep state misperception in paradoxical insomnia and boundary instability in narcolepsy type 1. Here, we present DyssomniaNN: a multi-scale biophysical computational framework coupling Borb\'ely's two-process regulation of sleep homeostasis and circadian drive with Saper's hypothalamic reciprocal flip-flop circuit and a recurrent corticothalamic leaky integrate-and-fire spiking neural network. We formalize five clinical cohorts across an identical 72-hour photoperiod: healthy control, circadian rhythm sleep-wake disorder (delayed-phase), chronic insomnia, paradoxical insomnia, and narcolepsy type 1. The synthetic local field potential converges closely with empirical human electroencephalography spectra from the PhysioNet Sleep-EDF database across all canonical frequency bands (spectral mean absolute error of 0.33--0.35). cross Goal-Oriented Bundle Management and Flow Control for Deep-Space Communications via Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Sajjad Baghaeea, Aimin Lib, Shanmugarajan Balasubramanian, Elif Uysal

Abstract: Deep-space communications operate under intermittent connectivity, long propagation delays, and relay storage constraints. In such networks, the Bundle Protocol enables store-carry-forward communication, requiring relay nodes to manage limited storage and scarce contact opportunities. Relay-side scheduling and dropping decisions are inherently goal-oriented: transmission resources should be allocated to bundles most valuable to downstream mission objectives. Conventional schedulers, such as FIFO, do not explicitly optimize task utility and may provide unfair service across heterogeneous planetary sources. This paper presents a closed-loop modeling and learning framework for relay-side bundle and flow management in a contact-driven deep-space network, where lunar and Martian updates are forwarded through a shared-buffer relay to Earth. We quantify destination-side information freshness using excess age $\Delta_i^e(t)=[\Delta_i(t)-d_i(t)]^+$, where $\Delta_i(t)$ denotes the Age of Information of source $i$ and $d_i(t)$ is a source-dependent delay baseline. Based on this metric, we define a goal-oriented loss that supports source-dependent nonlinear penalties, allowing prioritization according to mission relevance. We formulate relay control as a constrained semi-Markov decision process (CSMDP) and solve it using masked Lagrangian Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Feasibility-aware action masking restricts transmissions to bundles that can complete within the remaining contact window. DTNSim simulations indicate improved age-based utility and inter-flow fairness compared with baseline schedulers. The proposed framework jointly optimizes relay-side bundle scheduling, dropping, and flow control under contact-driven dynamics with explicit fairness considerations across heterogeneous sources.

cross Constructive Bounds for the Topological Expressivity of Neural Networks

Authors: Kosio Beshkov

Abstract: The expressivity of a neural network $\Phi$ describes the class of functions that a network can express. The term topological expressivity refers to a family of measures particularly suited to understand binary classification and is often formulated in terms of the Betti numbers of a decision boundary $\beta (\Phi^{-1}(0))$. Despite its popularity, this measure is not as useful for regression and other problems that do not involve a decision boundary. Instead, we propose to study topological expressivity in terms of the Betti numbers of the output of a neural network $\beta(\Phi(X))$. We formally prove that a ReLU network with architecture $(d,\sum\limits_{k=1}^{d } \beta_k(3k+2),\sum\limits_{k=1}^{d}\beta_k(k+1))$ can express spaces homotopy equivalent to wedge sums of spheres with arbitrary Betti numbers. This gives upper bounds for any arbitrary connected ($\beta_0=1$) sequence of Betti numbers, but by constructing spaces homotopy equivalent to products of $N$ spheres of dimension $d_1,...,d_N$ we achieve much better bounds $(\sum\limits_{n=1} d_n, 3\sum\limits_{n=1}\limits^N d_n + 2N, \sum\limits_{n=1}\limits^N d_n + N) $ for Betti numbers given by $\prod_n (1+x^{d_n})$. Finally we conjecture a much stronger tight bound which gives the architectural bound $(d,d+1,d+1)$ for a space homeomorphic to the sphere.

cross Hide and Seek: HIDE Concepts to SEEK explanations for black box models

Authors: Bhanu Tokas, Rahul Nair, Rockwell Jackson, Hannah Kerner

Abstract: Interpreting machine learning model predictions is a central challenge, particularly for black-box vision models. Concept-based attribution methods interpret models using human-understandable concepts. Existing concept-based methods can only interpret classification and regression models. They require access to model weights or large concept-annotated datasets, and most methods cannot provide image-level attribution. We propose Hide and Seek (HnS), a new method for concept-level attribution that does not require access to model weights or labeled concept datasets. HnS can interpret a wide range of vision models. Given a concept and image set, HnS retrieves concept-aligned images using a vision-language model, localizes the concept within each image, masks the localized region, and measures attribution from the change in the black-box model's output between the original and masked images. This enables faithful attribution by directly measuring the model's behavior and supporting local attributions. We validated HnS on CelebA and COCO-Stuff. Compared to existing methods, HnS showed better agreement with ground-truth attributions. We demonstrated the applicability of HnS on diverse tasks, including image captioning and satellite image segmentation, where no existing concept attribution method can be used. We provided our anonymous code in the supplementary material.

replace Linearized Wasserstein dimensionality reduction with approximation guarantees

Authors: Alexander Cloninger, Keaton Hamm, Varun Khurana, Caroline Moosm\"uller

Abstract: We introduce LOT Wassmap, a computationally feasible algorithm to uncover low-dimensional structures in the Wasserstein space. The algorithm is motivated by the observation that many datasets are naturally interpreted as probability measures rather than points in $\mathbb{R}^n$, and that finding low-dimensional descriptions of such datasets requires manifold learning algorithms in the Wasserstein space. Most available algorithms are based on computing the pairwise Wasserstein distance matrix, which can be computationally challenging for large datasets in high dimensions. Our algorithm leverages approximation schemes such as Sinkhorn distances and linearized optimal transport to speed-up computations, and in particular, avoids computing a pairwise distance matrix. We provide guarantees on the embedding quality under such approximations, including when explicit descriptions of the probability measures are not available and one must deal with finite samples instead. Experiments demonstrate that LOT Wassmap attains correct embeddings and that the quality improves with increased sample size. We also show how LOT Wassmap significantly reduces the computational cost when compared to algorithms that depend on pairwise distance computations.

replace TernaryLLM: Ternarized Large Language Model

Authors: Tianqi Chen, Zhe Li, Weixiang Xu, Zeyu Zhu, Dong Li, Lu Tian, Emad Barsoum, Peisong Wang, Jian Cheng

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance on Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks, but they are hindered by high computational costs and memory requirements. Ternarization, an extreme form of quantization, offers a solution by reducing memory usage and enabling energy-efficient floating-point additions. However, applying ternarization to LLMs faces challenges stemming from outliers in both weights and activations. In this work, observing asymmetric outliers and non-zero means in weights, we introduce Dual Learnable Ternarization (DLT), which enables both scales and shifts to be learnable. We also propose Outlier-Friendly Feature Knowledge Distillation (OFF) to recover the information lost in extremely low-bit quantization. The proposed OFF can incorporate semantic information and is insensitive to outliers. At the core of OFF is maximizing the mutual information between features in ternarized and floating-point models using cosine similarity. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our TernaryLLM surpasses previous low-bit quantization methods on the standard text generation and zero-shot benchmarks for different LLM families. Specifically, for one of the most powerful open-source models, LLaMA-3, our approach (W1.58A16) outperforms the previous state-of-the-art method (W2A16) by 5.8 in terms of perplexity on C4 and by 8.2% in terms of average accuracy on zero-shot tasks.

replace Provably Accurate Shapley Value Estimation via Leverage Score Sampling

Authors: Christopher Musco, R. Teal Witter

Abstract: Originally introduced in game theory, Shapley values have emerged as a central tool in explainable machine learning, where they are used to attribute model predictions to specific input features. However, computing Shapley values exactly is expensive: for a general model with $n$ features, $O(2^n)$ model evaluations are necessary. To address this issue, approximation algorithms are widely used. One of the most popular is the Kernel SHAP algorithm, which is model agnostic and remarkably effective in practice. At the time of our original submission, no strong non-asymptotic complexity guarantees were known for Kernel SHAP. We address this issue by introducing Leverage SHAP, a lightweight modification of Kernel SHAP that provides provably accurate Shapley value estimates with just $O(n\log n)$ model evaluations. Our approach takes advantage of a connection between Shapley value estimation and agnostic active learning by employing leverage score sampling, a powerful regression tool. Beyond theoretical guarantees, we find that Leverage SHAP achieves an approximately $50\%$ reduction in error compared to the highly optimized implementation of Kernel SHAP in the widely used SHAP library [Lundberg & Lee, 2017].

replace Instance-Level Difficulty: A Missing Perspective in Machine Unlearning

Authors: Hammad Rizwan, Mahtab Sarvmaili, Hassan Sajjad, Ga Wu

Abstract: Current research on deep machine unlearning primarily focuses on improving or evaluating the overall effectiveness of unlearning methods while overlooking the varying difficulty of unlearning individual training samples. As a result, the broader feasibility of machine unlearning remains under-explored. This paper studies the cruxes that make machine unlearning difficult through a thorough instance-level unlearning performance analysis over various unlearning algorithms and datasets. In particular, we summarize four factors that make unlearning a data point difficult, and we empirically show that these factors are independent of a specific unlearning algorithm but only relevant to the target model and its training data. Given these findings, we argue that machine unlearning research should pay attention to the instance-level difficulty of unlearning.

replace Deep End-to-End Survival Analysis with Temporal Consistency

Authors: Mariana Vargas Vieyra, Pascal Frossard

Abstract: In this study, we present a novel Survival Analysis algorithm designed to efficiently handle large-scale longitudinal data. Our approach draws inspiration from Reinforcement Learning principles, particularly the Deep Q-Network paradigm, extending Temporal Learning concepts to Survival Regression. A central argument in our method relies on temporal consistency, a recursive consistency condition between survival predictions made at consecutive states. Our framework introduces a delayed target network that decouples the generation of temporally consistent training targets from model updates, providing a more stable training signal while enabling mini-batch optimization and end-to-end training. Additionally, it supports arbitrarily complex architectures, enabling the modeling of intricate temporal dependencies. Through numerous experiments we provide empirical evidence demonstrating our framework's ability to exploit temporal consistency across datasets of varying sizes. Moreover, our algorithm outperforms benchmarks on datasets with long sequences, demonstrating its ability to capture long-term patterns. Finally, ablation studies show that the degree of target delay has a substantial effect on predictive performance and training variability. Across two distinct survival model families, delayed target updates consistently improve over the same-timescale baseline.

replace Conditional Vendi Score: Prompt-Aware Diversity Evaluation for Generative AI Models and LLMs

Authors: Mohammad Jalali, Azim Ospanov, Amin Gohari, Farzan Farnia

Abstract: Generative models guided by text prompts are widely evaluated for fidelity and prompt alignment, yet their ability to produce diverse outputs remains underexplored. Existing diversity metrics such as Vendi and RKE, which are based on the von Neumann and R\'enyi entropies of kernel matrices, were developed for unconditional models and cannot distinguish prompt-induced from model-induced variability. We address this gap by introducing \textit{Conditional-Vendi} and \textit{Conditional-RKE}, diversity measures derived from the conditional entropy of positive semidefinite matrices. These scores isolate model-induced diversity in prompt-guided generation, with Conditional-RKE enjoying an $O(1/\sqrt{n})$ convergence rate. For Conditional-Vendi, we introduce a truncated-spectrum approximation that yields scalable and consistent estimates. Experiments on text-to-image, image-captioning, and LLM tasks show that the conditional scores recover ground-truth diversity orderings and can also guide diffusion models toward more diverse samples. The codebase is available at https://github.com/mjalali/conditional-vendi.

URLs: https://github.com/mjalali/conditional-vendi.

replace Bandits with Anytime Knapsacks

Authors: Eray Can Elumar, Cem Tekin, Osman Yagan

Abstract: We consider bandits with anytime knapsacks (BwAK), a novel version of the BwK problem where there is an \textit{anytime} cost constraint instead of a total cost budget. This problem setting introduces additional complexities as it mandates adherence to the constraint throughout the decision-making process. We propose SUAK (Strategic Under-utilization for Anytime Knapsacks), an algorithm that utilizes upper confidence bounds to identify the optimal mixture of arms while maintaining a balance between exploration and exploitation. SUAK is an adaptive algorithm that strategically utilizes the available budget in each round in the decision-making process and skips a round when it is possible to violate the anytime cost constraint. In particular, SUAK slightly under-utilizes the available cost budget to reduce the need for skipping rounds. We show that SUAK attains the same problem-dependent regret upper bound of $ O(K \log T)$ established in prior work under the simpler BwK framework, where $K$ denotes the number of arms, and $T$ denotes the time horizon. We also establish an instance-dependent lower bound showing that the $O(K\log T)$ dependence is unavoidable. Finally, we show via extensive simulations that SUAK achieves considerably higher rewards than naive adaptations of BwK algorithms that do not optimize learning under anytime cost constraints.

replace Disentangling Total-Variance and Signal-to-Noise-Ratio Improves Diffusion Models

Authors: Khaled Kahouli, Winfried Ripken, Stefan Gugler, Oliver T. Unke, Klaus-Robert M\"{u}ller, Shinichi Nakajima

Abstract: Diffusion models have been widely adopted as a powerful generative framework across numerous scientific domains, yet their inherently iterative sampling process remains a significant computational bottleneck. While this is typically mitigated by reducing the number of reverse diffusion steps, sample quality becomes highly sensitive to the chosen noise schedule, i.e. the manner in which noise is introduced and the signal is reduced at each step. Although prior work has improved on the original variance-preserving and variance-exploding schedules, these approaches passively adjust the total variance, without direct independent control over it. In this work, we propose a novel total-variance/signal-to-noise-ratio disentangled (TV/SNR) framework, where TV and SNR can be controlled independently. Our approach reveals that schedules with exponentially exploding TV can be consistently improved by adopting a constant TV schedule while preserving the underlying SNR schedule. Furthermore, we demonstrate that generalizing the SNR schedule of optimal transport flow matching significantly enhances generation efficiency. Crucially, these gains remain consistent across various reverse diffusion solvers and reduce the search space to two independent and interpretable parameters, enabling fast and robust sampling for complex scientific applications, such as molecular structure generation.

replace EmbodiTTA: Resource-Efficient Test-Time Adaptation for Embodied Visual Systems

Authors: Xiao Ma, Young D. Kwon, Dong Ma

Abstract: Continual Test-time adaptation (CTTA) continuously adapts the deployed model on every incoming batch of data. While achieving optimal accuracy, existing CTTA approaches present poor real-world applicability on resource-constrained edge devices, due to the substantial memory overhead and energy consumption. In this work, we first introduce a novel paradigm -- on-demand TTA -- which triggers adaptation only when a significant domain shift is detected. Then, we present OD-TTA, an on-demand TTA framework for accurate and efficient adaptation on edge devices. OD-TTA comprises three innovative techniques: 1) a lightweight domain shift detection mechanism to activate TTA only when it is needed, drastically reducing the overall computation overhead, 2) a source domain selection module that chooses an appropriate source model for adaptation, ensuring high and robust accuracy, 3) a decoupled Batch Normalization (BN) update scheme to enable memory-efficient adaptation with small batch sizes. Extensive experiments show that OD-TTA achieves comparable and even better performance while reducing the energy and computation overhead remarkably, making TTA a practical reality.

replace Heterogeneity-Aware Client Sampling for Optimal and Efficient Federated Learning

Authors: Shudi Weng, Chao Ren, Ming Xiao, Mikael Skoglund

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) commonly involves clients with diverse communication and computational capabilities. Such heterogeneity can significantly distort the optimization dynamics and lead to objective inconsistency, where the global model converges to an incorrect stationary point potentially far from the pursued optimum. Despite its critical impact, the joint effect of communication and computation heterogeneity has remained largely unexplored, due to the intrinsic complexity of their interaction. In this paper, we reveal the fundamentally distinct mechanisms through which heterogeneous communication and computation drive inconsistency in FL. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first unified theoretical analysis of general heterogeneous FL, offering a principled understanding of how these two forms of heterogeneity jointly distort the optimization trajectory under arbitrary choices of local solvers. Motivated by these insights, we propose Federated Heterogeneity-Aware Client Sampling, FedACS, a universal method to eliminate all types of objective inconsistency. We theoretically prove that FedACS converges to the correct optimum at a rate of $O(1/\sqrt{R})$, even in dynamic heterogeneous environments. Extensive experiments across multiple datasets show that FedACS outperforms state-of-the-art and category-specific baselines by 4.3%-36%, while reducing communication costs by 22%-89% and computation loads by 14%-105%, respectively.

replace Optimizable Model Averaging for Ensembling Heterogeneous Model Outputs

Authors: Mijung Park

Abstract: Foundation model ensembles can improve accuracy and robustness, but full fine-tuning and parameter-space merging are often expensive or impossible when candidate models have different architectures. We propose Optimizable Model Averaging (OMA), a black-box approach that learns nonnegative model-combination weights, one scalar coefficient per model, to average the candidate models' predictive probabilities, leaving all model parameters frozen, by minimizing regularized predictive entropy. A central challenge is that minimizing predictive entropy can favour confidently incorrect models: increased confidence need not imply improved accuracy or calibration. We therefore examine regularization as a mechanism for controlling adaptation and empirically evaluate its effect on calibration, observing lower expected calibration error in selected configurations, alongside better performance-calibration trade-offs than model output averaging. As foundation models continue to advance, these approaches will enable the incorporation of future, potentially much better models to improve performance on challenging text and image classification tasks.

replace Regression-adjusted Monte Carlo Estimators for Shapley Values and Probabilistic Values

Authors: R. Teal Witter, Yurong Liu, Christopher Musco

Abstract: With origins in game theory, probabilistic values like Shapley values, Banzhaf values, and semi-values have emerged as a central tool in explainable AI. They are used for feature attribution, data attribution, data valuation, and more. Since exact computation can require exponential time, research has focused on efficient approximation methods using two techniques: Monte Carlo sampling and linear regression formulations. In this work, we present a new way of combining both of these techniques. Our approach is more flexible than prior algorithms, allowing for linear regression to be replaced with any function family whose probabilistic values can be computed efficiently. This allows us to harness the accuracy of tree-based models like XGBoost; with cross-fitting, the method also produces unbiased estimates. From experiments across eight datasets, we find that our methods give state-of-the-art performance for estimating probabilistic values. For Shapley values, the error of our methods can be $11\times$ lower than Permutation SHAP (the most popular Monte Carlo method), $6.5\times$ lower than Kernel SHAP (the most popular linear regression method), and $3.4\times$ lower than Leverage SHAP (the prior state-of-the-art Shapley value estimator). For more general probabilistic values, we can obtain error $390\times$ lower than the best estimator from prior work.

replace User Trajectory Prediction Unifying Global and Local Temporal Information

Authors: Wei Hao, Bin Chong, Ronghua Ji, Chen Hou

Abstract: Trajectory prediction is essential for formulating proactive strategies that anticipate user mobility and support advance preparation. Therefore, how to reduce the forecasting error in user trajectory prediction within an acceptable inference time arises as an interesting issue. However, trajectory data contains both global and local temporal information, complicating the extraction of the complete temporal pattern. Moreover, user behavior occurs over different time scales, increasing the difficulty of capturing behavioral patterns. To address these challenges, a trajectory prediction model based on multilayer perceptron (MLP), multi-scale convolutional neural network (MSCNN), and cross-attention (CA) is proposed. Specifically, MLP is used to extract the global temporal information of each feature. In parallel, MSCNN is employed to extract the local temporal information by modeling interactions among features within a local temporal range. Convolutional kernels with different sizes are used in MSCNN to capture temporal information at multiple resolutions, enhancing the model's adaptability to different behavioral patterns. Finally, CA is applied to fuse the global and local temporal information. Experimental results show that our model reduces mean squared error (MSE) by 5.04% and mean absolute error (MAE) by 4.35% compared with ModernTCN in 12-step prediction, while maintaining similar inference time.

Authors: Yulong Yang, Jiayin Liu, Vineet Bansal, Christine Allen-Blanchette

Abstract: Mechanics-inspired neural dynamical models improve interpretability and physical consistency, but they usually assume a known configuration space, fixed physical parameters, and deterministic prediction from a prescribed initial condition. replace Multidimensional Bayesian Active Machine Learning of Working Memory Task Performance

Authors: Dom CP Marticorena, Chris Wissmann, Zeyu Lu, Dennis L Barbour

Abstract: While adaptive experimental design has outgrown one-dimensional, staircase-based adaptations, most cognitive experiments still control a single factor and summarize performance with a scalar. Two variables are controlled: spatial load L (number of occupied tiles) and feature-binding load K (number of distinct colors) of items. replace Mixture of Many Zero-Compute Experts: A High-Rate Quantization Theory Perspective

Authors: Yehuda Dar

Abstract: This paper uses classical high-rate quantization theory to provide new insights into mixture-of-experts (MoE) models for regression tasks. Our MoE is defined by a segmentation of the input space to regions, each with a single-parameter expert that acts as a constant predictor with zero-compute at inference. Motivated by high-rate quantization theory assumptions, we assume that the number of experts is sufficiently large to make their input-space regions very small. This lets us study the approximation error of our MoE model class: (i) for one-dimensional inputs, we formulate the test error and its minimizing segmentation and experts; (ii) for multidimensional inputs, we formulate an upper bound for the test error and study its minimization. replace A Theory of the Mechanics of Information: Generalization Through Measurement of Uncertainty (Learning is Measuring)

Authors: Christopher J. Hazard, Michael Resnick, Jacob Beel, Jack Xia, Cade Mack, Dominic Glennie, Matthew Fulp, David Maze, Andrew Bassett, Martin Koistinen

Abstract: Traditional machine learning relies on explicit models and domain assumptions, often limiting flexibility and interpretability. We introduce a "model-free" framework of instance-based learning using surprisal (information theoretic uncertainty) to directly analyze and perform inferences from raw data, eliminating distribution modeling, reducing bias, and enabling efficient updates including direct edits and deletion of training data. By quantifying relevance through uncertainty, the approach enables generalizable inference across tasks including generative inference, causal discovery, anomaly detection, and time series forecasting. Our approach emphasizes traceability, interpretability, and data-driven decision making, offering a unified, human-understandable framework for machine learning, can be adapted to virtually all machine learning tasks, achieving competency across all and state-of-the-art performance on anomaly detection and some supervised tasks. replace Bayesian dynamic scheduling of multipurpose batch processes under incomplete look-ahead information

Authors: Taicheng Zheng, Dan Li, Jie Li

Abstract: Multipurpose batch processes become increasingly popular in manufacturing industries since they adapt to low-volume, high-value products and shifting demands. These processes often operate in a dynamic environment, which faces disturbances such as processing delays and demand changes. To minimise long-term cost and system nervousness (i.e., disruptive changes to schedules), schedulers must design rescheduling strategies to address such disturbances effectively. Existing methods often assume complete look-ahead information over the scheduling horizon. This assumption contrasts with realistic situations where schedulers can only access incomplete look-ahead information. Sticking with existing methods may lead to suboptimal long-term costs and high-level system nervousness. In this work we propose a Bayesian dynamic scheduling method. Our method relies on learning a Bayesian Network from the probability distribution of disturbances. Specifically, the Bayesian Network represents how likely each operation will be impacted by disturbances. During the online execution, when new disturbances become observed, this method updates the posterior distribution and therefore guides the rescheduling strategy. We compare our method with the existing periodic rescheduling strategy (which generates new schedules from scratch at fixed intervals) on four benchmark problems. Computational results show that our method achieves statistically better long-term costs and system nervousness. In the theoretical aspect, we prove that if disturbances are mutually independent, the impact-quantifying variables inherently satisfy the independence assumptions required by Bayesian Networks. As an implication, practitioners can extend the method to other scheduling problems (such as job shop scheduling and continuous processes), given that they define the problem-specific dependencies between operations.

Authors: Deepthi Pathare, Leo Laine, Morteza Haghir Chehreghani

Abstract: A central difficulty in applying reinforcement learning to real-world applications such as autonomous driving is that conventional scalar reward formulations obtained by aggregating multiple often competing objectives may obscure the structure of their trade-offs. These challenges are particularly relevant to decision-making for heavy-duty vehicles, which requires balancing safety, efficiency, and operational costs under demanding commercial requirements. We present a preference-conditioned multi-objective reinforcement learning framework that explicitly represents these objectives and utilizes Generalized Policy Improvement and Proximal Policy Optimization to learn policies capturing different trade-offs between them. The proposed method approximates the Pareto front across three conflicting objectives: safety, quantified in terms of collisions and successful completion; energy efficiency, quantified using energy cost; and time efficiency, quantified using driver cost. We evaluate the framework in a scalable highway traffic simulation environment tailored for tactical decision making in heavy-duty trucks. The preference-conditioned policy enables transitions between different driving behaviors without retraining, demonstrating the potential of multi-objective reinforcement learning for adaptive decision making in autonomous heavy-duty vehicles.

replace Beyond Prediction-Set Size: Quantifying Conformal Uncertainty through Credal Geometry

Authors: Siu Lun Chau, Soroush H. Zargarbashi, Yusuf Sale, Michele Caprio

Abstract: Prediction-set size offers a coarse summary of conformal uncertainty. We develop a richer perspective through the geometry of credal sets: families of probability distributions compatible with conformal prediction (CP) regions. We establish this representation for split CP, extending a correspondence recently identified for full CP. Building on this representation, we derive analytical characterisations and exact algorithms for several widely used credal uncertainty measures, reducing potentially exponential computations to linear time after sorting the conformal p-values. We further unify several of these measures with classical conformal summaries by integrating set-size penalties based on Tsallis entropies across error levels. We also prove an impossibility theorem: for common split-conformal regression constructions, uncertainty summaries of the induced credal sets cannot distinguish between inputs. Classification experiments on selective prediction and distribution-shift detection show that these summaries often recover useful ranking information lost by prediction-set size at a fixed error level, enabling finer-grained uncertainty assessment. These results establish credal geometry as a principled framework for quantifying conformal uncertainty and understanding its limitations.

replace COBALT: Contextual Bandit Learning with Offline Trajectories for Multi-Turn Code Generation

Authors: Ziru Chen, Dongdong Chen, Ruinan Jin, Yingbin Liang, Yujia Xie, Huan Sun

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) for multi-turn code generation typically relies on online reinforcement learning (RL), in which models learn from repeatedly generated online rollouts. Although effective, online RL can be costly and unstable. In this paper, we present contextual bandit learning with offline trajectories (COBALT), a simple method that reuses offline multi-turn trajectories to efficiently train LLMs for multi-turn code generation. Building on the observation that multi-turn code generation is a one-step recoverable Markov decision process, COBALT first collects code generation trajectories using a reference LLM and divides them into partial trajectories. Then, during online bandit learning, the LLM is trained to complete each partial trajectory through single-step code generation.
